The launcher icon and the native splash accent are orange too (`src-tauri/icons`, the Android mipmaps, `csdrive_accent`).

**The picker is a dialog.** Settings → Appearance shows only a summary (the theme, the mode, the rotation if it is on) and a *Change…* button that opens the dialog (`AppearanceDialog`): the light/dark buttons, the rotation controls and every theme as a card, grouped by family. It keeps up with a change made anywhere else (`subscribeAppearance`).

**Rotation** (`Rotation` `{ enabled, mode, unit, every, generated }`, `appearance.rotation` in `global_settings`) goes through *all* the themes, in the catalog's order (**ascending**, wrapping), backwards (**descending**) or at random (**random**: never the theme that is showing) — or, with `generated.enabled`, makes up its own colours instead (below), in which case `mode` is ignored. It is done by the backend, so it goes on whatever is on screen: a task looks once a second whether the interval has passed (`rotate_if_due`), moves on, saves the theme (or draws a fresh generated colour) and tells every window; a restart starts the clock again. **Choosing a theme by hand doesn't stop it and the picker is never disabled**: the rotation goes on from the theme chosen, its interval starting again. The interval is **seconds, minutes, hours or days** and a whole number of them, and **never shorter than 30 seconds** (`MIN_INTERVAL_SECS`; at most a year): every change recolours the whole window, and anything faster is tiring to look at — a few minutes or more is comfortable. The dialog only sends an interval that is valid and says why when it isn't; the backend refuses one anyway. **A change fades**: the colours slide over in about 0.6 s (`.theme-fade` on the root element for 0.7 s, a css transition of the colour properties) — never a flash — and not at all for a person whose device asks for less motion. Rust-tested (`next_theme` both ways and random, the interval, the list against the catalog) and live on desktop: a web app choosing the theme and Notes and the admin-app following, the refusals (a web app or Notes setting the rotation, 29 seconds), the dialog, and a real rotation of 30 seconds that moved on to the next theme by itself while the theme was also chosen by hand.

**Generated colours** (`Rotation.generated: { enabled, spread }`, Settings → Appearance → the rotation box's own checkbox): instead of the catalog, each step draws a fresh colour scheme near the **next of six key hues** — Red, Yellow, Green, Teal (cyan), Blue, Magenta, evenly spaced round the RGB colour wheel (`appearance::KEY_COLORS`/`KEY_COLOR_NAMES`, fetched by the frontend with `key_color_names` rather than kept as a second copy) — always forward, never ascending/descending/random (those only mean something for a fixed list). **`spread`** (0–255, default 64, "How close" in the dialog) is added or subtracted **independently to each R/G/B channel** of the key colour (clamped to 0–255) — not a hue-wheel offset: `0` always lands on the key colour exactly, and a bigger spread lets one channel drift toward a neighbour's while another stays put. From that one drawn RGB, `appearance::palette_for` builds a full light **and** dark palette the way a hand-made theme has one: the background is the drawn colour alpha-blended over white (light, ~10%) or black (dark, ~22%), kept low enough that the background stays close to white/black exactly like every catalog theme's does; **`fg` and `accentFg` are computed, not guessed** — whichever of black/white contrasts more with the background (WCAG relative luminance) — because a fixed hex value picked for one hue can't be assumed to read well against a background any of 256×256×256 possible draws could produce; `accent` is the raw, un-blended drawn colour (vivid, unlike the background); `muted`/`border`/`panel`/`hover` are plain grey steps between the background and the text colour, neutral chrome around a coloured background and vivid accent — the same four-colour shape every catalog theme's own swatch shows (bg/panel/accent/fg). The drawn palette is computed and persisted **once**, in Rust (`appearance.generated`, a `GeneratedPalette` — `{ keyIndex, light, dark }` — in `global_settings`, separate from `appearance.theme`, which stays a real catalog id the whole time so turning generated colours back off falls back to the theme chosen before they were turned on), and sent to every window inside the same `appearance-changed` event (`Appearance.generated: Option<GeneratedPalette>`) — never redrawn independently per window, which is what makes every window agree on the exact same colour rather than each rolling its own dice. **Turning it on draws one at once** (so the dialog never shows "on" with nothing chosen); merely re-saving other rotation settings while it's already on — the interval, the spread — leaves the *current* colour alone, since re-drawing on every settings save would change the colour someone is looking at for no reason they asked for; the new spread takes effect on the *next* drawn colour (the next rotation tick, or turning generated colours off and on again, which always restarts the cycle at Red). **The theme catalog grid is dimmed and inert** (`.theme-family-disabled`, `pointer-events: none`, every card also `disabled`) while generated colours are on, since picking a catalog theme then would have no visible effect until they're turned off again — confusing silence otherwise. Rust-tested (`appearance.rs`): a channel's offset never leaves `[key-spread, key+spread]` ∩ `[0,255]` and `spread=0` always lands exactly on the key; the six keys are distinct, fully-saturated RGB primaries/secondaries; drawing cycles them in order; **every key colour, at spreads 0/64/128/255 and several random draws, in both modes, clears WCAG AA (4.5:1) for text on the background and the button-text minimum (3.6:1) for text on the accent** — the whole reason `fg`/`accentFg` are computed rather than fixed; old persisted rotation JSON without a `generated` field still deserializes (off, default spread). Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop): the checkbox, the "How close" spread box and the six-name legend (fetched from the backend) appearing only while it's on; enabling it applied a real palette (`data-theme="generated"`, `--bg`/`--accent`/`--fg` all present and, in dark mode, `--accent` staying the same while `--bg`/`--fg` swapped for the dark variant); the catalog grid actually inert (all 58 cards `disabled`, all 18 families dimmed); spread 0 drawn fresh landed on the *exact* key RGB (`#ff0000`); a real 30-second rotation advanced the drawn colour by itself (confirmed two steps forward, Red → Green, over the elapsed wait) with the theme catalog untouched; and a second window (Notes), opened mid-rotation, agreed exactly with the admin window's current colour — the same broadcast palette, not two independent draws.

**A window that misses the live event never used to catch up on its own — reported live on Android** ("Generate colours… changes instantly when I click that option but then stays on that same theme forever"): the person's own description — the *first* draw always applied correctly, but a window left alone afterward (locked screen, switched to another app) never advanced again even much later. **The backend loop itself was never the problem**: live testing on the emulator (isolated data, a real 30-second rotation, direct `get_appearance` reads over CDP) confirmed `rotate_if_due` keeps ticking and persisting correctly the whole time regardless of what any window is doing, and — on the stock emulator — the `appearance-changed` push event *did* keep landing and reapplying correctly even across a real 90-second backgrounding, both continuously-foregrounded and background-then-resume. So the exact failure mode on a real device (most likely a WebView whose renderer the OS suspended or killed while backgrounded — Tauri's own generated Android `RustWebViewClient` has no `onRenderProcessGone` handler at all, checked directly in `gen/android`, so a dead renderer would simply never run the JS that applies a pushed update again) couldn't be reproduced end-to-end on this emulator/WebView version. Rather than chase a device-specific reproduction further, the fix closes the gap **regardless of the reason a push was missed**: `initAppearance()` (`src/lib/appearance.ts`) now also listens for `visibilitychange` and, the moment the page becomes visible again, reads `get_appearance` fresh and reapplies it — the same self-healing shape a window already gets at startup, just re-run every time the person actually looks at the page again, not only once. This covers every window that calls `initAppearance` (the admin-app, Notes, the PDF viewer) in the one shared module, with no per-window changes needed. Verified live on the Android emulator (isolated data folder): backgrounding the app for 75 seconds during a real 30-second rotation and resuming it (confirmed the *same* devtools page, not a fresh reload) showed the correct, fully caught-up colour; to prove it was genuinely this new code path and not just the push event happening to still work, the DOM's `--bg` was hand-corrupted to a fake stuck value and a synthetic `visibilitychange` dispatched with nothing else changing in between — the page corrected itself back to the real current backend colour within half a second, which only the new resync code could have done.

**The fix above was re-verified on a real device, not just the emulator, after the person clarified their report** ("I never said the app freezes… it gets stuck on the first generated theme") and asked to test it directly: a debug build (isolated data, its devtools socket reachable over `adb forward` — release builds disable WebView debugging entirely, by Android's own design, so a *release* build can't be inspected live this way, only a debug one) was installed on a real Samsung Galaxy A55 (Android 16, WebView ~153/140 — a far newer engine than the emulator's stock 113), connected over CDP. **A real 3-second rotation advanced correctly while the app stayed continuously in the foreground** (`keyIndex` observed moving 0→1→3→4→5→wrap→1→2 over six polls, the DOM's `--bg` matching the backend's own answer at every single check) — so the core mechanism was never broken on real hardware either when actively watched. **The background-then-resume case was also re-verified on this same real device**: backgrounded for 25 seconds during the rotation, resumed (confirmed the same devtools page, not a reload), and the on-screen colour had already caught up to the backend's current state the moment it was checked — the fix holds on real Samsung hardware, not only the emulator's older engine. The signed **release** build (the one actually left running on the phone) carries the identical source as the debug build that was tested this way; release mode couldn't be checked live the same way (no devtools socket at all once installed as a release build), so that specific binary's behaviour is inferred rather than independently observed — nothing about a release compile plausibly changes how either the Rust async loop or a JS event listener behaves, but this is recorded honestly as the one part of this fix not observed end-to-end.

**The rotation's minimum interval was lowered from 30 seconds to [`MIN_INTERVAL_SECS`] = 1 second** (`appearance.rs`, `appearance.ts`'s `MIN_ROTATION_SECONDS`, and `AppearanceSettings.tsx`'s copy, all three kept in step as they already were), at the person's own request specifically so a rotation can be watched happening in seconds while testing, rather than needing to wait out a multi-minute interval to see whether it's working. The floor of 1 (not 0) still exists only so "how often" is always a positive whole number of seconds — the earlier "faster than that is tiring to look at" framing is gone from the docs and the dialog's own copy, since a comfortable everyday interval is now left entirely to the person choosing it rather than enforced as a rule. Rust-tested (`the_interval_is_checked_and_never_shorter_than_one_second`: a 1-second interval succeeds, a 0-second one is refused with a correctly-singular "at least 1 second." message) and exercised live as part of the real-device verification above (the 3- and 2-second intervals used there wouldn't have been legal before this change).

**A generated palette's own `accent` could be nearly unreadable as plain text on a light background — reported live** ("themes that use bright shades centered around green as foreground text color render the text unreadable on light theme"), specifically about the randomly generated palettes, not the hand-made catalog. The cause: `accent` is deliberately the **raw, un-blended** drawn key colour (see "Generated colours" above) — a fine, vivid *background* for a button, where `accentFg` is computed against it directly — but the app's own CSS also uses `--accent` as **literal text/icon colour**, straight on the page's own background, in a handful of places that predate the generated-colours feature entirely: `.link-button:hover`/`.link-button.active` and `.group-toggle:hover`'s hover/active text, `.tab-text-bullet` (the separator between a tab label's pieces), and Notes' `.notes-badge-checkout`/`.notes-badge-notebook` badges (`App.css`, `notes.css`). None of that was ever contrast-checked against `bg`, because a *catalog* theme's own `accent` is a hand-picked value that already happens to work as text (never verified automatically, just never a saturated primary either) — only a generated palette's `accent` can be a full-saturation hue like Green or Yellow, both close to white's own luminance, which reads fine on a dark background (green-on-black is the classic high-contrast pairing) but is close to invisible on a light one.

**Fixed with a second, computed colour — `accentText`/`accent_text` — used only for what `accent` was never meant to guarantee.** `appearance::readable_variant_of(rgb, bg)` (`appearance.rs`) returns `rgb` unchanged the moment it already clears the same 4.5:1 bar `fg`/`bg` does — true for most key/mode combinations — and otherwise blends it, in small steps, toward whichever pole (black or white) is farther from `bg`'s own luminance, stopping the instant the bar is cleared rather than diluting it any further than it has to. `ThemeColors` (Rust and its TypeScript counterpart in `appearance.ts`) gained the new field — a generated-palette-only addition, since a catalog theme's own `Palette` (`themes.ts`) has no such thing and needs none. The five CSS spots above now read `color: var(--accent-text, var(--accent))` — a plain CSS fallback, so a catalog theme (which never sets `--accent-text` at all) falls straight through to its own `--accent`, **completely unchanged**; only `applyAppearance` (`lib/appearance.ts`), the one place that ever *sets* `--accent-text`, does so while `appearance.generated` is present, and removes it the moment a catalog theme is chosen instead. Border-only accent usages (`border-color: var(--accent)`, including the same two Notes badges' own borders) were deliberately left alone — a thin border isn't body text, WCAG's own bar for it is much lower, and the report was specifically about text.

Rust-tested (`appearance.rs`): `a_generated_palettes_text_always_contrasts_with_its_background` now also asserts `accentText` clears 4.5:1 against `bg` for every one of the six keys, every spread (0/64/128/255) and every random draw, in both modes — the same exhaustive sweep `fg`/`accentFg` already had; a dedicated test confirms a colour that already reads fine (Blue on a near-white background) is returned bit-for-bit unchanged, while one that doesn't (Green on the same background) is genuinely adjusted, clears the bar afterward, and is never blended all the way to black. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, a real running app over its WebView2 CDP port — not a hand-computed check): forced light mode and a fast (spread-0) rotation, landed on a high-luminance key (Teal/cyan, the same class of problem as Green) and read the real applied CSS — `--accent` stayed the vivid, unchanged `#00ffff`, `--accent-text` came out as a properly darkened `#008080`, and a throwaway `.tab-text-bullet` element's own `getComputedStyle(...).color` resolved to that same darkened value, not the raw vivid one. The literal reported colour (Green) wasn't caught live at the exact instant by name — the rotation ticked past it during the check — but it's covered by the exhaustive Rust sweep above, which the live-checked mechanism is the same code path for.

**The fix above was incomplete — reported live again, by exact key name this time** ("The problem persists, and it does so for yellow, green and teal (so not for red, blue or magenta)" — precisely the three keys whose luminance is above 0.5, matching this bug's own reasoning exactly). One more place used the raw `accent` as literal text, missed by the original sweep because it isn't one of the CSS spots that reads `--accent` directly at all: `themes.ts`'s `variablesOf` sets **`--tok-heading` to `c.accent` unconditionally**, for *every* theme, catalog or generated — it's the colour of a markdown heading, a link's text and a function name in the syntax-highlighted editor (`App.css`'s `.tok-heading`/`.tok-linktext`/`.tok-function`). Unlike the CSS spots already fixed, this one couldn't be caught by a `var(--accent-text, var(--accent))` fallback, because `variablesOf`'s loop in `applyAppearance` sets `--tok-heading` to a **literal colour value**, not a `var()` reference — nothing to fall back *through*. Fixed by having `applyAppearance` override `--tok-heading` straight after that loop, to the same `accentText` already computed for `--accent-text`, whenever `appearance.generated` is present; a catalog theme's `--tok-heading` is left exactly as the loop set it (its own `accent`, unchanged — there is no independent value for it anywhere in the catalog, so nothing regresses). Verified live the same way as before (isolated data folder, desktop, real WebView2 CDP): forced light mode, a spread-0 rotation landed on Green (`keyIndex` 2) — `--accent` stayed the raw vivid `#00ff00`, `--accent-text` **and** `--tok-heading` both came out as the same darkened `#008000`, and a throwaway `.tok-heading` element's own `getComputedStyle(...).color` resolved to `rgb(0, 128, 0)` — the readable value, not the raw one; turning generated colours back off (a real catalog theme, Ayran orange) showed `--accent-text` removed (empty, so CSS's own fallback takes over) and `--tok-heading` back to exactly `#d04a0b` — the catalog theme's own accent, bit-for-bit unchanged.

**Saving a custom theme** (asked for directly: "there is still no way to save a randomly generated theme"). A generated draw is otherwise ephemeral — gone the moment the rotation moves on, or the app closes — so **"Your saved themes"**, a new grid in `AppearanceDialog` right below the "Generate colours…" controls (shown only once at least one exists, the same minimal-when-empty rule `TagList` already follows), is the one way to keep one: each saved theme is a `CustomTheme` (`appearance.rs`: `{ id, name, light, dark }`, `id` a fresh `custom-<uuid>`, never one of the catalog's own slugs, so the two id spaces can never collide) persisted as a JSON list under `appearance.customThemes` in `global_settings` — a backend concept from the start, not a frontend-only list, since the catalog itself is compiled into every build and a "custom" theme is the only kind that can be *added* at runtime at all. `save_custom_theme(name, light, dark)`, `list_custom_themes()` and `delete_custom_theme(id)` are granted in `user-apps.json` like `set_appearance` itself — any window, the same trust level as choosing a theme already has, since saving one doesn't apply it, only remembers it for picking again later. The colours a page hands in are validated server-side (`validate_theme_colors`/`hex_color_valid`: each must be a plain `#rrggbb` string) before ever being trusted into a saved record or a CSS variable, the same caution `asHexColor` already takes in the other direction.

**"Don't forget that the previously generated random theme should also be possible to save."** By the time someone notices a nice draw and opens the save dialog, a short rotation interval may already have moved on — so `Appearance` gained a second field, `previousGenerated` (`appearance.rs`'s `PREVIOUS_GENERATED_KEY`, `appearance.previousGenerated` in `global_settings`): the one draw the rotation just moved on *from*, kept for exactly one step of history, not a running log. It's updated only by a **real rotation tick** (`rotate_if_due`, right before overwriting `GENERATED_KEY` with a fresh draw) — never by merely re-saving the rotation's other settings or by toggling generated colours off and on again, both of which correctly leave it `None` (nothing to offer yet, since the person hasn't watched an actual tick happen since). The dialog shows two small swatches and buttons side by side when both exist — *Save this colour scheme…* and *Save the previous one…* — each opening the same inline "name it" box (Enter to confirm, Escape to cancel) rather than a separate modal.

**A page that only calls `get_appearance`/listens for `appearance-changed` still resolves a custom theme correctly, with no separate fetch.** Unlike a catalog theme (compiled into every frontend build, resolved locally from a plain id), a custom theme's colours exist nowhere but its one saved database record — so `Appearance` gained a third field, `custom: Option<CustomTheme>`, sent inline whenever `theme` names a saved custom one, the exact same reasoning `generated` was already sent inline for. `applyAppearance` (`lib/appearance.ts`) resolves `appearance.generated ?? appearance.custom ?? themeById(appearance.theme)` — a generated draw always wins while the rotation is running (whatever `theme`/`custom` say is inert until it's turned off), and `--accent-text`/`--tok-heading` are set from whichever of the two (`activeColors`) is active, since a saved custom theme is just a kept copy of some past generated draw and carries the exact same need for a readable-as-text accent stand-in a catalog theme's own hand-picked `accent` never did.

**Deleting a saved theme that's currently active falls back at once, everywhere, with no reload.** `delete_custom_theme` removes it from the list and then unconditionally re-broadcasts `appearance-changed` from a fresh `read()` — which, no longer finding that id among either the catalog or the (now-shorter) custom list, falls back to the default theme the same way an id from a since-removed app version already did. Each custom theme's card in the grid is a sibling pair — the plain `.theme-card` select button plus a small, always-enabled `Trash2` `IconButton` absolutely positioned at its corner (`.theme-card-wrap`/`.theme-card-remove` in `App.css`) — never nested, since two `<button>`s can't be.

**A real bug was found live, not by inspection, before any of this could be trusted**: the very first version had `previousGenerated` landing as `undefined` on the frontend no matter how many real rotation ticks had passed, confirmed by direct `get_appearance` polling over CDP showing the *backend's* `keyIndex` genuinely advancing every second while `hasPrevious` stayed `false` throughout. Cause: `Appearance` (`appearance.rs`) was serialized with no `#[serde(rename_all = "camelCase")]` at all — every field until this change (`theme`, `mode`, `dark`, `rotation`, `generated`) happened to already be a single word, so the mismatch between Rust's `previous_generated` and the frontend's expected `previousGenerated` was invisible until a genuinely multi-word field was added. Fixed by adding the attribute to the struct; re-verified afterward with the same direct-polling method, this time showing `hasPrevious: true` and the correct one-step-behind `keyIndex` from the very first tick onward.

Rust-tested (`appearance.rs`): `hex_color_valid` and `validate_theme_colors` on a full set of valid colours and one invalid field (named in the error); the existing reserved-key test extended to the two new keys. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real WebView2 over CDP): enabling rotation + generated colours at a 1-second interval and polling `get_appearance` directly showed `previousGenerated` correctly absent right after turning generated colours on (nothing to offer yet) and present with the correct one-step-behind `keyIndex` from the first real tick onward; saving both the current and the previous draw through the actual dialog (the inline name box, Enter to confirm) landed both in `list_custom_themes` with their real, validated hex colours; selecting a saved custom theme's card (with the generated rotation turned off, so the cards aren't disabled) applied its *exact* `--bg`/`--accent` and set `data-theme` to its own id; deleting the currently-active custom theme removed it from the list **and** flipped the live page back to the default `ayran-orange` theme immediately, with no reload, confirmed both through the backend's own `get_appearance` and the page's actual applied CSS; a saved theme survived a full restart of the isolated app (a fresh `list_custom_themes()` call after relaunch returned it byte-for-byte). `npm run build`, `cargo build`, and `cargo test --lib` (250 tests) all clean throughout.

**Editable seed colours** (asked for directly: "add an option to edit the list of colors that the randomly generated themes should be started from and even be able to save such lists and revert to the default — the 6 colors we currently have fixed"). `KEY_COLORS`/`KEY_COLOR_NAMES` are now only the **default** — what a fresh install starts from and what "Revert to the default colours" puts back — not the list a generated rotation actually draws from any more. The active list lives in `global_settings` (`appearance.keyColors`, a `Vec<KeyColor>` — `{ name, color }`, `color` a plain `#rrggbb` string like every other colour this module stores) and falls back to the compiled-in six (`default_key_colors()`) whenever nothing is saved or the stored value is empty/malformed — `read_key_colors` treats that the same as "nothing saved," never refusing to rotate. `generated_rgb`/`generated_palette`, previously closed over the `KEY_COLORS` constant directly, now take the colour list as a plain parameter (`&[(u8, u8, u8)]`) — `draw_generated` is the one place that reads the active list and converts it to RGB tuples (`from_hex`) before calling them, so the pure drawing functions stay trivially testable against *any* list, not just the default, and nothing about them has to know where the list came from. A list must have **at least one** colour (`generated_rgb`'s `% len()` would panic at zero) and at most `MAX_KEY_COLORS` (24); each entry needs a non-empty name (≤ `MAX_KEY_COLOR_NAME_LEN`, 40) and a real `#rrggbb` value (`validate_key_colors`, the same shape `validate_theme_colors` already has for a saved theme).

**Saved lists work exactly like saved themes, one level up** — a `SavedKeyColorList` (`{ id, name, colors }`, `id` a fresh `keycolors-<uuid>`) in `appearance.savedKeyColorLists`, the key-colour counterpart of `appearance.customThemes`. **Every mutating command is admin-app only** (`set_key_colors`, `save_key_color_list`, `delete_key_color_list` — `require_admin`, granted in `admin.json` only, the same trust level `set_appearance_rotation` already keeps to itself: editing the rotation's own seed colours is editing its configuration, not picking a theme to look at) — unlike `save_custom_theme`, which *is* open to any window, since saving a plain theme isn't configuring anything. The three read-only commands (`get_key_colors`, `get_default_key_colors`, `list_saved_key_color_lists`) are granted in `user-apps.json`, the same read-only trust level as `list_themes`/`list_custom_themes`. **Changing the active list takes effect on the *next* drawn colour, never the one on screen now** — the same "re-saving settings leaves the current colour alone" rule `spread` already follows — so editing the list while generated colours are on doesn't recolour the window out from under the person for no reason they asked for.

**Settings → Appearance → "Edit the seed colours…"** (`AppearanceSettings.tsx`, inside the existing generated-colours section, right below the legend that already named the six hues — now built from `getKeyColors()` instead of a hand-kept copy, so it reads correctly whatever list is actually active) toggles an editable list: one row per colour — a native `<input type="color">` swatch and a text name, a per-row remove button (disabled on the last remaining colour, so the list can never be emptied from here — the backend would refuse it anyway, but there's no reason to let the person hit that error) — plus **Add a colour**, **Revert to the default colours** (fetches `getDefaultKeyColors()` and applies it) and **Save this list…** (the same inline "name it" box pattern `save_custom_theme`'s own two "Save…" buttons already use). A colour's own swatch applies immediately on change (a discrete picker action, not continuous typing); its name is typed locally and committed on blur — the same "typed text doesn't fight the person, applied once settled" pattern the spread and interval boxes already use elsewhere in this same dialog. **"Your saved colour lists"** lists each saved list by name with up to six small swatches ahead of it (a feel for the list without a full preview) — pressing the name makes it the active list (`setKeyColors(list.colors)`), a trash button removes the saved entry (not the active list itself).

Rust-tested (`appearance.rs`): `from_hex`/`to_hex` round-trip; `default_key_colors()` matches the compiled-in six, name for name and RGB for RGB; `validate_key_colors` on empty, too many, an unnamed entry, a whitespace-only name, an over-length name, a bad hex value (the error names the offending entry by its own name), a valid default list and a valid single-colour list; `generated_rgb`/`generated_palette` drawing from a hand-built two-colour list — never touching `KEY_COLORS` — confirming the wrap is by *that* list's own length (2), not the compiled-in six's. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real WebView2 CDP, real mouse clicks and keyboard input throughout — not `.click()`/programmatic `.focus()`, which this environment's WebView2 silently no-ops without real OS window focus, confirmed by `document.hasFocus()` reading `false` after a plain JS `.focus()` call and `true` only once the window was genuinely brought to the OS foreground first): opened the editor and saw the real default six with correct swatches and names; removed Magenta (confirmed gone from `get_key_colors`, the other five untouched); added a colour (`#808080`, "Colour 6") and renamed it "Lavender", confirmed persisted; changed its swatch to a custom `#b57edc`, confirmed persisted immediately with no separate commit step; saved the resulting six-colour list as "Lavender Fields" (`list_saved_key_color_lists` showed it back byte-for-byte); **Revert to the default colours** correctly restored the stock six while leaving "Lavender Fields" untouched in the saved list; pressing the saved list's own name restored the custom six as the active list again. **The rotation itself was confirmed to actually draw from the custom list, not the compiled-in default** — the most important behaviour, not just the editor around it: with "Lavender Fields" active and a 1-second interval running, polling `get_appearance` caught `generated.keyIndex: 5` with `accent` reading the *exact* custom hex `#b57edc` — Lavender's own saved value, at its own correct index in a six-entry list — proof the active list genuinely drives `draw_generated`, not a lingering reference to the default six. `npm run build`, `cargo build` and `cargo test --lib` (267 tests) all clean throughout.


## Undo and redo in the editors

`components/CodeEditor.tsx` (the file editors and the note editor), `lib/undoHistory.ts`. Two buttons above the text — **Undo** and **Redo**, disabled when there is nothing to do — and **Ctrl+Z**, **Ctrl+Y** / **Ctrl+Shift+Z** (in `docs/keyboard-shortcuts.md`). The editor keeps its **own** history (the browser's is neither reachable from a button — Android's keyboard has no Ctrl+Z — nor reliable for a controlled box whose text is also replaced from outside): every change that goes through `onChange` is recorded — the clipboard menu's paste included —, typing within 800 ms is one step, at most 500 steps and 16 M characters (the oldest go first); a text replaced from outside (a file read again) starts a new history; the caret comes back with each step. The history is per editor instance: closing the editor or switching tab drops it (the unsaved text itself is kept as a draft). Node-tested (`undoHistory`) and live on desktop.


## Editor performance, and its settings: wrapping, whitespace, line numbers, Tab

**The lag reported on a big file, first fix**: `highlight()` (`lib/highlight.ts`) re-scanning the *entire* text synchronously, on every keystroke, inside the same render the keystroke caused. A first fix only changed *when* that scan ran (debounced 150ms after a pause, below a 20,000-character text) — the undo history retaining up to 500 steps/16 MB of a big file is a separate, likely contributor to "gets worse over time, better after reopening," but its bounds are deliberate and were left alone.

**Reported again, still lagging on a big file while typing continuously — the debounce fix didn't actually fix the complaint**: typing fast meant nothing was redrawn until the pause, because the box's own *visible* text is the highlighted copy drawn underneath the (transparent) real textarea, not the textarea itself — a debounce only moves *when* the expensive synchronous re-scan happens, not *where*, so a person who never pauses still watched nothing update at all. Asked for directly, with the architecture spelled out: move the real, exact re-scan off the main thread entirely, into a Web Worker (`lib/highlightWorker.ts`, a thin wrapper around the unchanged, DOM-free `highlight()`), coalesced by `lib/highlightScheduler.ts`'s `HighlightScheduler` — at most one re-scan ever running, and only the *latest* text waiting when one is busy, so a backlog of stale requests never builds up behind a fast typist; and show *something* correctly-ish coloured the instant each keystroke happens, before the Worker is even asked, rather than nothing until its answer arrives. `useBackgroundHighlighted` (`CodeEditor.tsx`, replacing the old debounce-based `useHighlighted`) is the result: the very first render is still computed synchronously on the main thread (a freshly opened file shows correctly coloured immediately, no flash of plain text while the Worker starts up); every change after that gets an **instant, approximate** stand-in — `tokenClassAtOffset` (`lib/highlight.ts`) reads which `tok-*` class was open, in the *last exact* highlight, at the caret's own position, and wraps the whole current text in that one flat colour (so typing inside a string keeps "string colour," inside a comment "comment colour," and so on) — shown via a `useLayoutEffect` (so it lands in the same paint as the keystroke, no flicker), while the real, exactly-retokenised pass runs in the Worker and replaces it the moment it's back.

**Every keystroke handler in `CodeEditor.tsx`'s textarea defers its own work into a `setTimeout(fn, 0)`**, asked for directly so that a keystroke's own native, instant feedback (the browser's default action — the caret and the character appearing) never sits in the same tick as this component's comparatively expensive side effects (recording an undo step, telling the parent, the layout effect above). The one exception, also asked for directly: a real *shortcut* (Tab, Ctrl+Alt+arrows/PageUp/PageDown, Ctrl+Z/Y) still decides **synchronously** whether it was actually hit and calls `preventDefault()` synchronously too (it has no effect once deferred) — only the shortcut's own *handling* (the edit, the scroll nudge, the undo/redo step) is deferred the same way `onChange` is. **Ctrl+K, 2 / Ctrl+K, 4 are deliberately left out of this**: they're `useChord` registrations, not this textarea's own `onKeyDown` — a two-key chord already waits on a second, separate keypress (at most 3s apart, see "Keyboard" above), so there's no native per-keystroke feedback for them to protect in the first place, and no hot path a backlog of these could ever build up on.

**A real, serious bug was found live by this deferral, not by inspection**: dispatching five real, back-to-back WebView2 keystrokes (CDP, no synthetic DOM events) into the textarea silently dropped four of them — only the first character ever made it into the final text. Root cause: the textarea was a React-*controlled* input (`value={value}`). Between a keystroke's own native DOM insertion (instant, since nothing prevents it) and its now-deferred `onChange` actually committing that text to React state, the DOM already held it — but if *anything* caused this component to re-render in that window (in particular, the *previous* keystroke's own deferred commit finishing and calling `redraw()`), React would force the controlled textarea's DOM value back down to the just-committed-but-already-stale state, erasing whatever a *later*, not-yet-committed keystroke had already typed natively in between. Entirely invisible in ordinary single-keystroke testing — it only shows up once more than one keystroke's deferred commit can be in flight across the same render — which is exactly what deferring `onChange` makes possible. **Fixed by making the textarea deliberately uncontrolled** (`defaultValue={value}`, not `value={value}`: applies only at mount) and syncing the DOM by hand, in the existing `[value]` `useLayoutEffect`, **only** when the change is one nothing else would have put into the DOM already — a `programmaticEditPending` ref, set just before `applyEdit` (Tab, chord indents, "Insert a path…") or `step` (undo/redo) call `onChange`, or the pre-existing "value doesn't match the history" external-reset check (a file re-read, a Filen version check). An ordinary keystroke's own deferred `onChange` echo trips neither condition, so the DOM — which the browser already updated natively, possibly with further keystrokes since — is left alone.

**Five settings, shared by every text editor in the app** (the Files tab's and Notes' alike) — `lib/editorSettings.ts`, stored in `global_settings` like `listPageSize`/`rowActionsCompact` (`editor.wrapLines`, `editor.showWhitespace`, `editor.lineNumbers`, `editor.tabInsertsSpaces`, `editor.tabSpaceCount`), with toggles in Settings → **Text editors**; `subscribeEditorSettings` follows `rowActionsCompact`'s own pattern, so every mounted editor updates the moment a setting changes in Settings, in the same window.

- **Wrap long lines** (on by default). Off, `CodeEditor.tsx` adds `code-editor-nowrap`: the content column (`.code-editor-content`, the flex item holding the highlighted `<pre>` and the `textarea`) switches to `white-space: pre` and grows to its own widest line (`width: max-content; min-width: 100%`) instead of shrinking to the frame's width, so the frame's own `overflow: auto` scrolls sideways. The line-number gutter's `position: sticky; left: 0` (below) keeps it in view through that scroll.
- **Show line numbers** (on by default) — `.code-editor-gutter`, a flex row (`.code-editor-rows`) beside `.code-editor-content`; `align-items: stretch` (the default) makes its box follow the textarea's own height, which `fit()` sets, not any CSS percentage chain. **Each line's number is individually positioned** (`.code-editor-gutter-number`, `top: <measured>px`), so a line that wraps across several visual rows (with "wrap long lines" on) still gets exactly one number, beside its first row — see "Wrapped-aware line numbers" below for how it's measured and the trade-off it replaced.
- **Show whitespace** (off by default) — `markWhitespace()` in `lib/highlight.ts` wraps every space, tab and non-breaking space of `highlight()`'s output in its own `<span class="tok-ws tok-ws-space|tab|nbsp">`, **unconditionally**, whatever the setting says: the setting only decides whether `App.css` styles those spans (`.code-editor-show-whitespace .tok-ws-… { … }`), so flipping it is a pure CSS change, never a fresh highlight pass — needed since the setting can change while an editor with a big file is already open. Runs only over the *text* pieces of already-highlighted HTML (split on `<[^>]+>`, which alternates with plain text in the string `highlight()` builds), so a tag's own attributes are never touched, keeping `highlight()`'s one rule: the text content doesn't change, only what it's wrapped in. **The tab's arrow and the non-breaking space's dot are an absolutely-positioned `::before` in CSS, never an inserted character** — the same reason nothing in `highlight.ts` uses synthesized italics (see its own module doc): a real character would add layout width and desync the highlighted overlay from the invisible textarea underneath it, character for character.
- **Tab / Shift+Tab indent and outdent** (`lib/editorIndent.ts`'s `applyTab`, pure text logic with no DOM) and **Ctrl+K, 2 / Ctrl+K, 4** (two/four tab-units at once, `docs/keyboard-shortcuts.md`). Reported live: Tab used to just move the focus to the next control, because a plain `textarea` doesn't intercept it — `CodeEditor.tsx`'s `onKeyDown` now calls `preventDefault()` and inserts the indentation by hand (`applyEdit`, which also records the undo step and restores the caret, exactly as a real keystroke would). A collapsed caret or a same-line selection replaces the selection with one tab-unit; every other case (outdenting always; an indenting selection spanning more than one line) works on **whole lines**, indenting/outdenting every line the selection touches and adjusting the selection to keep covering them. The Tab key inserts a real tab character, or `tabSpaceCount` spaces (1–16) when **"the Tab key inserts … spaces"** is on — either way, Shift+Tab treats that many columns of leading whitespace as one level to remove.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real WebView2 keystrokes over its CDP debugging port — not synthetic DOM events, since the point of the Tab fix is exactly whether the browser's own default action is actually suppressed): a real Tab press at the start of a line inserted a tab and **kept the textarea focused** (the reported bug, gone); Shift+Tab outdented it back; Tab and Shift+Tab on a multi-line selection indented/outdented both lines together and kept the selection over them; Ctrl+Z/Ctrl+Y still undo/redo those edits; Ctrl+K, 2 and Ctrl+K, 4 inserted two and four tab-units at the caret and across a multi-line selection alike. Turning "wrap long lines" off added a real horizontal scrollbar for a deliberately over-long line and turning it back on removed it (`scrollWidth`/`clientWidth` measured equal); the line-number gutter's `position: sticky` was confirmed to stay at the same screen position while the editor was scrolled horizontally by 400px; whitespace spans were confirmed present but visually inert (transparent background, no `::before` content) with the setting off, and showing a tinted background plus the dot/arrow glyphs with it on. `npm run build` and `cargo test --lib` (202 tests) both clean after all of the above.

**The Worker/deferred-keystroke rewrite above was verified live the same way** (isolated data folder, desktop, real WebView2 keystrokes dispatched over its CDP port — never synthetic DOM events, since what's under test is specifically how the browser's own default text-insertion action interacts with a controlled-vs-uncontrolled React textarea and a `setTimeout`-deferred `onChange`): on a 261,000-character synthetic file, five real keystrokes dispatched back-to-back landed all five characters, in order, in both the textarea and the highlighted overlay, with the overlay visibly catching up mid-burst (four of five characters already drawn within ~20ms, all five and the exact Worker-recomputed colouring settled within ~420ms) — this is the fix for the data-loss bug above, re-confirmed after it (an unpatched build, tested first, dropped four of the five characters every time). A single real keystroke inside a comment, and a realistic human-paced (~60ms between keys) "42" typed into a small file, both landed correctly with the caret where typing would leave it. Tab (inserted a real tab, stayed focused), Shift+Tab (outdented it back), Ctrl+Z and Ctrl+Y (undo/redo), and Ctrl+Alt+Down (no corruption, no thrown error) were all re-verified through the new deferred handlers specifically, not just the pre-existing Tab-only check above.

**Wrapped-aware line numbers** (asked for directly: a line long enough to wrap used to pull every number after it out of step, previously recorded above as "an accepted, documented trade-off… not a bug to chase" — revisited once actually asked for). `lib/wrappedLineRows.ts`'s `computeRowsPerLine` measures, for each source line, how many visual rows it actually occupies — **not** a character-count calculation (`Math.ceil(length / charsPerRow)`), which is subtly but confidently *wrong*: `white-space: pre-wrap` wraps at the last word boundary before the width limit, not at an exact character count, so a line whose last word would cross the limit wraps *earlier* than arithmetic predicts. The only reliable source is the browser's own layout engine, which has already wrapped the text correctly in `.code-editor-highlight`'s real DOM — `highlight()`'s own invariant (its module doc) guarantees a plain `TreeWalker` over its text nodes reads back exactly `value`, character for character, regardless of how many `tok-*`/`markWhitespace` spans wrap it — so one pass finds the (text node, offset) for every line's start, and a single collapsed `Range` at each (`getBoundingClientRect().top`) gives the exact pixel row it begins on. The gutter (`CodeEditor.tsx`) changed from one `<pre>` of `\n`-joined numbers to individually absolutely-positioned `<span>`s (`.code-editor-gutter-number`, `top: <measured>px`) — direct pixel positioning rather than building a text blob with blank filler rows for wrapped continuations, deliberately: the latter risks tiny cumulative rounding drift over a long file (each blank line's own `white-space: pre` row height compounding), where direct positioning ties every number to the exact same measurement with no accumulation possible. A `position: absolute` child is placed relative to its containing block's *padding* edge regardless of the padding itself, so `.code-editor-gutter` now carries no padding of its own — each span's `top`/`right` already adds the 8px offset by hand, matching `.code-editor-highlight`'s own top padding (both are `align-items: stretch` siblings in the same flex row, so their boxes start at the identical screen position, confirmed live before trusting the approach: `computeRowsPerLine`'s offsets, measured from `.code-editor-highlight`, transfer unchanged to `.code-editor-gutter`).

Measuring is real DOM work (`getBoundingClientRect` forces layout) and keeps a line-number gutter's own small size-based safety net (`MAX_LINES_FOR_WRAP_MEASUREMENT`, 20,000 — falls back to one row per line above it, the same "a bug isn't worth chasing past a point" judgement the rest of this section already makes elsewhere), and is deferred (`setTimeout`, not a `useLayoutEffect`) off `value`/`wrapLines` alone — not `html`, which also changes for the *approximate* and the *exact* highlight pass of one edit (`useBackgroundHighlighted` above): wrapping depends only on the text, never on which colour a span is drawn, so keying off `html` would measure twice for nothing. A **width** change (the window resized, a split pane dragged) can also change where every line wraps — the existing `ResizeObserver` that already drives `fit()` now also compares `contentRect.width` against its last value and re-measures only when it actually changed, not on every resize tick (most of which are height-only, from typing, and already handled by `fit()`).

`lib/wrappedLineRows.ts` splits out `lineStartOffsets` (pure, Node-tested against a handful of strings and cross-checked that its count always matches `value.split('\n').length`, the same count `CodeEditor.tsx`'s own `lineCount` already uses) from the DOM-dependent walking and measuring, which needs a real browser. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP): a file mixing short lines with two ~300-character lines (wrapping into 4 visual rows each at the test window's width) showed line 3's number appearing 78px (4 × the measured 19.5px row height) below line 2's — correctly skipping its wrapped rows, not immediately after it — matching a screenshot of the same file pixel-for-pixel; extending a short line until it also wrapped shifted every number after it down by exactly the right amount on the very next edit; turning "wrap long lines" off made every number fall back to flat `19.5px` spacing (one row each); and narrowing the editor's own frame from its full width to 350px re-triggered measurement through the resize path alone (no edit, no setting change) and shifted the later numbers to reflect the new, narrower wrapping. `npm run build`, `cargo build` and `cargo test --lib` (250 tests) all clean.


## Searching and sorting (Notes' File Manager, Note Files Explorer and lists of notes)

**The panel** (*Search and sort…* in the toolbar of a file manager and in the header of a notes page; `SearchPanel.tsx`; the logic is `search.ts`, plain functions and generators, tested in Node with an in-memory source: terms, contents, intervals, sorting, the walk, the pager, notes). **Search by**: **name** (a file's or folder's name, a note's title), **contents** (a file's text, a note's markdown), **last modified** and **created** time stamps and **size** — each an *interval* whose either edge may be left empty (infinity) —, **extension** (a list such as `jpg, png`), and **type** (files / folders). **Every text term can be a regular expression**; a regular expression on the contents is applied **line by line** (`^`/`$` are a line's) or **to the whole contents** (a newline is then the expression's business). A plain term is a case-insensitive substring unless *Match case*. **Depth**: 1 (this folder only) by default, any number of levels, or *No limit* (never deeper than 100, links can make a tree endless). A bad regular expression, an interval that ends before it starts or a wrong depth is refused with a message before anything runs. Enter in a box searches.

**Sorting** (the same panel; effective at once): by name (numbers as numbers), extension, last modified, created or size — *As listed* for files is folders first by name; for notes it is **by index** —, ascending or descending, folders first or not; a value nobody knows (a size, a date) goes last in either direction. Sorting a folder of this device by size or date reads them for the whole folder in **one call** (`fs_read_dir_detailed`: size, modified and created time per entry — `FileSource.listDetailed`; the plain listing has names only, and sizes are read for the page on screen as before); a Filen listing has size and modified time already, no creation time (the panel says so), and a folder of this device may not keep one either.

**Nothing is ever collected in memory.** A search is an *async generator* (`searchFiles`, `searchNotes`) that walks the tree folder by folder — one listing at a time — and yields one hit at a time; a `ResultPager` (`useResultPager`) pulls from it **only as far as the page on screen needs** and keeps the last six pages: the next page continues the walk, a page that was let go is found again by walking again (slower, never more memory), and Stop is just leaving (clearing, changing folder, closing). So a search that finds a million things holds a page or two, whatever the tree. What that costs is the **order**: results are **grouped by folder** — the folder's own hits (sorted as asked) first, then each subfolder's, depth first — and each says which folder it is in (**its ancestors**: the folder's path for files and folders, the titles of the notes above it for a note); a *global* sort of a deep search would need every hit at once, so it is exact within each folder and — at depth 1 — everywhere. The status line says how many were found so far (the total once the whole tree was looked at), how many folders were looked in, and how many files could **not be searched**: contents are read only from files of up to **10 MiB** that are text (no NUL in the first 8 000 characters), so nothing big is pulled into the page for a search, and a Filen file is fetched to the cache only if it is small enough (its size is in the listing).

**Results** replace the listing while a search is on (the folder's pagination and thumbnails give way; `FileSearchResults`, `NoteSearchResults`): a file result shows its name, its folder, the line that matched (`line 3: …`), size and date, opens like a listed file, and *Show it in its folder* leaves the search and lists that folder with the file focused; a note result shows its index, title, the notes above it and the matching line, and offers the web app, the markdown, its children and *show it among the notes next to it*. Changing folder clears the search. **While a list of notes is searched or sorted, everything that reorders, renumbers or converts indexes is off** — the indexes dialog, editing an index (the badge, Shift+F2, the menu item) and *normalize the remaining notes* in Delete: what is in view is not in the order those operations act on (`indexOps`).

Tested live on desktop (isolated data): sorting by size, date (folders first kept), name; searches by name (case), contents at every depth (line and folder shown, the big and the binary file reported), regular expressions line by line and on the whole contents, modified / created / size intervals with an edge left out, extension, a refused expression, a 60-file folder paged 25 at a time (next, next, previous, *Show it in its folder*); notes — sort by title and created, the index tools going off, a title and a contents search at every depth with the parent titles, the list coming back on clear. Tested on the Android emulator too (isolated data): the panel fits the phone's width with no sideways scroll, a contents search at every depth (each result with its folder), sorting by size through the one detailed listing, and the indexes dialog's interval buttons ticking a whole interval and converting it. **Not run live:** a search or sort in a Filen account (the cache's listings feed the same generator; it is covered by unit tests only) and the notes search on the Android emulator (verified on desktop).

## Links planted in `user/`

A directory junction or symlink planted inside `user/` (web apps can't create one — no command does — but the OS user, an unzip or a `git clone` can) is **refused in every direction**: the scope follows it to its real location, so listing, reading or creating files through a link that leads outside is an error (this used to be a gap with the fs plugin, which only resolved paths that already existed). The link itself can still be removed or renamed from inside, and its target is never touched. Tested with real symlinks (unit tests) and a Windows junction (live). On Android the `user` folder is in the app's private storage, so only `adb run-as` on a debug build (or root) can plant a link at all.

## Building and running (the admin-app is the frontend)

`csdrive-webhost-tauriapp` is a standard Tauri project: the frontend (the admin-app: React + Vite + TypeScript) at its root and the Rust backend in `src-tauri/`. `tauri.conf.json` points `frontendDist` at `../dist`, which Tauri embeds into the binary at compile time, so it is in every installer with no separate file and no network access — and there is no way for a user or a web app to swap it out, because it isn't a file in the data folder. (Its saved UI state is keyed by `layout::ADMIN_APP_ID`.)

- **Develop:** `cd csdrive-webhost-tauriapp && npm install && cargo tauri dev` — Vite on `:1420` with hot reload (`devUrl`), the app loading it, `devCsp` in force.
- **Installer:** `cargo tauri build` (runs `npm run build` first, via `beforeBuildCommand`, and turns on Tauri's `custom-protocol` feature so the embedded frontend is used). Android: `cargo tauri android build …` (see "Android").
- **Multi-page build:** `vite.config.ts` builds one html page per app — the admin-app (`index.html`) and each system app (`system/<id>/index.html`, see "System apps and Notes") — into `dist/`; Tauri embeds them all.
- **Run the embedded frontend without an installer:** `npm run build`, then in `src-tauri`: `cargo run --features tauri/custom-protocol`. A plain `cargo run` is Tauri's *dev mode*: it loads `devUrl` and needs Vite running — that is Tauri's normal behaviour.
- `cargo build`/`check`/`test` need `dist/index.html` to exist (Tauri embeds it at compile time); `build.rs` stops with the command to run if it doesn't.

## Web apps opened from Notes

Notes can open an **html or markdown file** as a web app — from the user folder, a folder the person picked, or a Filen account (in a branch too): the **Open as web app** button on such a file in its file list (`FileSource.openAsWebApp`, `open_file_as_web_app`; any window may call it — Notes' pages, and web apps too since the system apps' rights are the web apps' — and it needs a registered tab of the caller to list the page under).
- **Serving** (`notes_pages.rs`; the `csuser://` protocol is now *asynchronous* and percent-decodes the path): besides the user folder, two address spaces — `/@device/<root id>/<path>` (a picked folder, judged by the file scope like any other path) and `/@filen/<user id>/<branch index or ->/<path in the drive>` (through the Filen cache: fetched if not cached or expired, then served from the cache; a file the branch changed is served as the branch has it). A page's **relative references** (a stylesheet, an image, a script, a link — `../` included) resolve against its own address, so they are answered from the same place; an address in the reserved `/@…` space that names nothing valid is refused. Markdown is rendered as described below whatever it is served from. Nothing in these addresses is a real path.
- **The window** (when it is a window of its own — see below for a tab of the Notes window) is an ordinary window entry (`kind` user) whose path is `@device/…` or `@filen/…` (`validate_relative_html_path` lets those through — they are judged when served) and whose `origin` records how it came to be opened (`Origin`: `openedBy` `NotesApp`, `storage`, the path in that storage, the picked folder's root id, the Filen account with the branch's name and index), told to the page in its init response. It has a `parent_tab`: the Notes tab that opened it.
- **Listed under the Notes tab that opened it**, not among the apps (`list_secondary_windows` leaves children out; "close all"/"suspend all" and the shutdown include them): in the admin-app the tab's level of *opened from this tab* lists them together with the external web sites (`TabRecord.openedApps` next to `externalPages`, oldest first, reorderable within the tab; an item shows an app-window icon, the page's own label — its first tab's — and its storage and path, with tags on the window's guid and focus / suspend / reopen / close of the window).
- **Siblings — any web page can open another page, in a window of its own.** `open_related_web_app(path)` (`openRelatedWebApp`; `TabLib.openPage(path)` in the samples): `path` is relative to the page's own — the page its window is showing now, which a link may have changed (`join_relative`: `.`, `..`, query and fragment handled, never above the root; a leading `/` is from the root of its storage), resolved in the same storage (folder, picked folder, drive and branch). A page that was opened from Notes gives the new page **the same parent tab** — it appears beside the page that asked, under the Notes tab; any other page of the user folder gives it a window of the apps list (`secondary_windows::open_page_window`, like one opened from the admin-app). Nothing asks the person: it is a page's own doing — unlike a *link*, which the person is asked about (`link_navigation`). (A note's page can also be a *tab of the Notes window itself* — see "A web page inside a system window"; the window's rights then follow the page it shows.)
- **External web sites** asked for by such a page (or by Notes itself) are listed **directly under that Notes tab** (`open_external_site` uses the caller window's `parent_tab` when it has one; the events still go to the asking page).
- **Lifecycle.** They are children: **suspending the Notes window suspends them** (and, through their own destruction, their external sites); **closing/deleting the Notes tab, its group or its window closes and deletes them** — recursively, with their tabs, tags and external sites (`delete_window_and_tags` → `notes_pages::Closing`).
- **Links.** A click on a link to the web in such a page opens the OS browser (see "No network access…"); a link to another page of the same folder, picked folder or drive is put to the person — open it in this tab, in a new tab, or copy its address (next section).
- Tested live on desktop: a markdown file of a picked folder (its `../theme.css`, an image and a script), a sibling, an external site from such a page listed under the Notes tab, a link click that left the window where it was, suspending and closing the Notes tab; and, on the real Filen account with a scratch folder, an `.md` file whose stylesheet came from the cache, a sibling `.html` using `../style.css`, and the same page opened from a branch (the branch's text, `branch: "selftest-pages"`).

## Markdown web apps

A `.md` / `.markdown` file can be opened as a web app exactly like an `.html` file (Files tab → "Open as web app", the User Apps tab) — `validate_relative_html_path` accepts it. Unlike an html file, whose bytes are served as they are, the `csuser://` handler (`serve_file` in `lib.rs`) turns a markdown file into html on the Rust side first (`markdown.rs`, `pulldown-cmark` with tables, footnotes, strikethrough and task lists) and sends the resulting **whole page**: a charset, a viewport, a small default stylesheet (light/dark), the document, and a script at the end.
- **Raw html passes through untouched** (markdown is html-compatible), so `<style>` and `<script>` tags in the file work — under the same policy as every web app (inline script and style allowed, no `eval`, no network).
- **Relative references** (a `<link rel=stylesheet href>`, `@import`, `<script src>`, an image, a link to another `.md`) are ordinary requests for a path relative to the page's address, answered by the same handler from the same folder — for html pages as much as markdown ones. (An address outside the app — a CDN — is refused by the CSP: an app needing network goes through a backend command.) Following a link to another `.md` shows that page in the same window.
- **The page is a tab** like a hand-written app: the last script registers it (`init_window_tab`), applies the code snippets, and labels it with the document's title (its first heading, else the file name) and path, and sets its resource id to the page's path plus query — so what the window manager shows follows the page. A tab switched to while it is showing starts the page over.
- **The scroll stays pinned to the bottom across a reload, if it was there before one.** The note editor's own auto-sync (`notify_file_saved`) reloads this exact page on every save — a plain `location.reload()` only restores the *same pixel offset*, which stops being the bottom the moment the edit made the document taller (the usual case, text appended at the end), so the page looked like it "lost" the scroll on every sync. The bootstrap script now records, in `sessionStorage` (`beforeunload`, so it captures the position right before any reload — a sync, a tab switch back to the page, or a manual reload; there's no meaningful difference between them, only whether the person was at the end), whether the page was scrolled to its bottom, and — `sessionStorage` survives a `location.reload()` of the same document, and is cleared only when the window itself closes, so it's exactly this page's own lifetime — scrolls back to the (new) bottom right away and again once every resource (an image) has finished loading and may have changed the page's height.
- Tested: title, table, an inline `<style>` and `<script>`, a relative stylesheet, a relative link, the tab label and the init response, on desktop; the scroll-pinning script's presence and shape (Rust unit test), and — see "Editor ↔ syncing web app: scroll and refresh options" below — live, as a regression check: appending text and saving landed the page at the *new* bottom, not the old pixel offset.

**A note opened as a web app now draws its own auto-hiding top bar** (asked for directly: this page has none of "The top bar" above for free — it's a genuinely different page, outside Notes' own React tree, served over `csuser` rather than being one of Notes' own views). `markdown.rs`'s `BOOTSTRAP` script builds it: the same four actions `csdrive-webhost-userapps/example-toolbar/index.html` demonstrates as the reference shape (Back — `window_go_back` —, Suspend this window, Close this tab, Close this window), the tab's own `topBarHtml` (its label/tags/root markup, free from the `csdrive-tb-*` code snippet this page already applies), and its own × — plus, on top of that, scroll-driven collapse ported from `lib/scrollAutohide.ts`'s `useScrollAutohide` to plain JS (simpler here: one scrollable area, the whole document, not several independently-scrolling regions in a React tree).

**It starts shown, not hidden** — every other page's top bar defaults to "hidden until asked for" (`tabs.top_bar_hidden`, following the global `topBar.autohide` setting), a sensible default for a bar the person can always summon again, but backwards for a page whose whole point is *auto*-hiding, where the person expects to see it first and have it hide as they scroll (reported live, after it shipped following the same "hidden by default" convention as everything else). Tracked separately, in this page's own `app_state` (keyed by the note's own file path, like any web app's — `HIDDEN_KEY = 'csdrive-top-bar-hidden'`), rather than the shared `tabs.top_bar_hidden` column every other page's bar reads (that column already means "follow the global default" for those pages, and changing what it means there would have changed their behaviour too, not just this new one's) — purely additive: nothing about an existing page changed, and a note's own bar has no persisted choice until the person actually closes or re-shows it once. `show-top-bar` (the admin's "Show the top bar") still reveals it exactly as for every other page's bar.

**Two distinct feedback-loop mechanisms were found live getting the scroll-driven collapse right, not one** — both start the same way: the bar's own collapse shortens the page, since (unlike `.editor-top-bar`'s `position: sticky` inside a flex row) it sits in *ordinary block flow* directly above the markdown content.
1. **Pinned at the very bottom** (the same mechanism "Top bar autohide and nested scrollable content" above already covers for the React-tree pages): if `scrollY` was already at the document's own maximum, shortening the page lowers that maximum below the current position, and the browser clamps `scrollY` down to fit — a genuine `scroll` event, read as "scrolled up," that would otherwise show the bar again (shortening the page back, clamping again, repeating). Guarded identically: a negative delta is ignored specifically while the page is still pinned at its own maximum scroll position.
2. **CSS scroll anchoring, found live afterward** ("I see a flicker," reported while that first fix was already verified working at the bottom edge) — on by default, it actively adjusts `scrollY` to keep the same content visually in place whenever something *above* the viewport changes height, which the bar's own collapse does from essentially *any* scroll position once it's scrolled out of view, not only the bottom edge — a second, more pervasive echo the pinned-at-bottom guard was never meant to catch. `overflow-anchor: none` (`STYLE`, on both `html` and `body`) opts the whole page out of scroll anchoring entirely — the standard, purpose-built way to do this — closing the loop at its source rather than trying to extend the delta-based guard to cover every position it could occur at.

Rust-tested (`markdown.rs`): the bar's own markup and the same four backend commands `example-toolbar` uses; `persistedHidden` starting `false` and reading/writing `get_app_state`/`set_app_state` under its own key rather than `tab.topBarHidden`; the pinned-at-bottom guard's exact condition; and `overflow-anchor: none` present on both `html` and `body`. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP) on an actual note opened from a real notebook (`DeskTestNB`), extended to ~6,300px of scrollable content: the bar started shown with the correct title/path in `topBarHtml`; scrolling down hid it and back up showed it again; a mid-document jump that previously produced 19+ drifting `scroll` events (`scrollY` oscillating down from ~600 toward the 550s over half a second, never settling) now produces exactly one; jumping straight to the document's own maximum — the original "the shake" reproduction — produced zero toggles (a `MutationObserver` on the bar's own `class` confirmed it), both before and after adding the mid-document fix, confirming the two guards don't interfere with each other; scrolling back up and clicking the bar's own × correctly hid it and persisted that choice (confirmed via a direct `get_app_state` read, value `"1"`) across a real `Page.reload()`.

**The editor's own "Show its top bar" button** (`NoteEditPage.tsx`, beside "Refresh the web app") — reported live, on real production use: a note's own web app window is deliberately *not* listed anywhere in the System/User Apps tab (see "Web apps opened from Notes" — it's a child of the Notes tab that opened it, not a window of its own there, and the person explicitly wants to keep it that way), so once its top bar is closed there was no row to press "Show its top bar" on the way there is for an ordinary secondary window — and `show_top_bar` itself is admin-only (`admin.json`), unreachable from `system:notes` anyway, which has exactly a web app's own rights. `notes_pages::sync_show_top_bar(file)` is the fix: the same shape as the sibling `sync_scroll_to_line`/`sync_scroll_nudge`/`sync_refresh_without_scroll` commands right above it — find the window(s) actually following `file` (`secondary_windows::syncing_windows`) and emit directly to them, this time `show-top-bar` — rather than reusing `show_top_bar`'s own admin-only path. No backend persistence needed on this end: the markdown page's own bootstrap script already persists the choice itself (its own `app_state`) the instant it receives the event, exactly as it already does for the admin's own "Show the top bar." Granted in `user-apps.json` like its three siblings (none of which have a dedicated unit test of their own either — `syncing_windows`/`emit_if_open`, the primitives underneath, are the tested layer). Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP): opened a real note's editor, opened it as a web app, closed the web app's own top bar (confirmed `app_state` read back `"1"`) — then, from the editor alone, with no row anywhere else to do it from, pressed the new button: the web app window's bar reappeared (`height: 41`, no hidden class) and the choice persisted (`app_state` back to `"0"`); with the web app window closed instead, the same button correctly showed "This note has no open web app to show it on." rather than silently doing nothing.

## The ayran tag: inserting html elements into rendered markdown

`ayran_tags.rs` (a module the doc comment there covers in full — read it first for the exact rule; this
section is the shorter, CLAUDE.md-level summary). A configurable way to make a markdown file's own source
insert a *real* html element into the page it renders to (`markdown.rs`'s `render_page`, so only a `.md`
file opened as a web app — never `render_fragment`, the internal-docs path `help_docs.rs` uses, which is
never a person's own content). The person writes a **pair** of one configurable element (`AyranConfig`:
`tag_name`, default **`a-x`**) with two configurable attributes — an **internal id** (`id_attr`, default
**`a-y`**, a plain string used only to match a tag to its partner) and a **css-selector-like** one
(`selector_attr`, default **`a-z`**, e.g. `div#wrap.card` — an optional tag name, then any number of
`#id`/`.class` segments in either order; empty defaults the tag to `div`):
```markdown
<a-x a-y="1" a-z="section#main.card" data-extra="kept"/>

some *markdown* content

<a-x a-y="1"/>
```
Two `tag_name` elements are a **matched pair** when they are **siblings** in the *rendered* markup and
their `id_attr` values are **equal** — everything between them becomes the children of a **new** element
(its tag/id/class parsed from the *first* tag's `selector_attr`; every other attribute of the first tag,
`id_attr`/`selector_attr` themselves aside, copied onto it as it is) that replaces the pair. Pairs can
**nest** (leaf pairs — the ones that don't themselves contain another complete pair — resolve first, so an
outer pair's already-resolved inner content just comes along as ordinary children). A tag that matches
nothing, matches **more than one** same-named sibling with the same id, shares an id with a same-named tag
that **isn't** a sibling, or genuinely **crosses** another pair (`A…C…B…D`, interleaved rather than nested
or disjoint — not one of the spec's own listed cases, but there's no sensible wrapping for it either) is
left exactly where it is: same tag, same attributes, same position, still a literal `<a-x>` — deliberately
the same outcome for every case this can't cleanly resolve, so a markup mistake shows up as visible `<a-x>`
tags rather than a silently wrong transform. **An empty `tag_name` disables the whole feature** — `apply`
becomes a no-op (a cheap substring check, nothing parsed or reserialized) — and the Settings page's own
text box for it says so.

**Why `a-x`, not `_x`.** The module is built on **`html5ever`** (`markup5ever_rcdom` for a mutable,
reserializable tree) — the same spec-compliant parser Firefox/Servo use, chosen deliberately so the
*surrounding* markdown/html a person writes is handled exactly as a real browser would, not by a
hand-rolled parser's own guesses. A real HTML5 tag name must *start with an ASCII letter* — verified live
against html5ever: `<_x ...>` is not tokenized as a tag at all, just left as inert literal text — so an
underscore-led name would silently never fire the feature for anyone using the default. `a-x`/`a-y`/`a-z`
(a letter, then a hyphen — exactly the shape a real "custom element" name must have) parse as an ordinary,
if unknown, element.

**Why self-closing needs its own pass first** (`rewrite_self_closing`, a plain text scan for occurrences of
*just* the configured tag name — quote-aware, so a `>`/`/` inside a quoted attribute value doesn't end the
tag early — leaving everything else in the markup completely unexamined). `<a-x a-y="1"/>` is *not* an
empty element under real HTML parsing — verified live: the trailing `/` is honoured only for the small set
of actual *void* elements (`br`, `img`, `hr`, …); for anything else it's silently ignored and the tag
swallows every following sibling as its own children until an explicit `</a-x>` or an ancestor implicitly
closes it — which would nest the second tag of a pair *inside* the first instead of beside it, breaking the
whole sibling-matching model. This pass turns `<a-x a-y="1"/>` into the equivalent explicit empty element
`<a-x a-y="1"></a-x>` before html5ever ever sees it, so self-closing tags — "most probably" how they'll be
written — still come out as two real siblings. One consequence: an untouched (unmatched/ambiguous/crossing)
tag is still always shown in its *paired* form afterward, even if it was written self-closing — same tag,
same attributes, same position, just always normalized syntax.

**Composing with `markdown.rs`'s own `data-line` mirror-scroll wrapping** was the one real integration
bug found building this: `body_with_line_anchors` wraps *every* top-level block — an HTML block included —
in its own `<div data-line="N">`, and a bare ayran tag written as its own paragraph (blank lines around it,
exactly the form the module doc describes) is exactly such a top-level block. Verified live: two markers
each wrapped in their *own separate* `data-line` div are not siblings of each other at all (each is the
sole child of a *different* div), so nothing could ever be recognised as matching — the whole feature would
silently do nothing for the natural way anyone would write it. Fixed by teaching `body_with_line_anchors`
(now taking the ayran config too) to recognise, via `ayran_tags::is_bare_tag`, a top-level HTML block that
is *purely* one ayran tag (open, self-closing or not, or close) and skip its `data-line` wrapper entirely
(an ayran marker has nothing of its own worth scrolling to anyway) — `render_page` then runs
`ayran_tags::apply` over the whole wrapped body afterward, so the two markers land as genuine top-level
siblings alongside the (still individually `data-line`-anchored) content between them.

**A second real bug, found by its own test suite, not live**: the very first version of the
"which candidate pairs cross" check compared each candidate only against *already-accepted* ones, in the
order `HashMap::into_values()` happened to hand them out — Rust's hash-map iteration order is randomized
per process, so which one of two genuinely crossing pairs got accepted (and wrongly collapsed) versus
rejected depended on that unspecified order, not on anything about the markup itself. Fixed by checking
every candidate against *every other* candidate up front (`ranges_cross`, symmetric, order-independent) and
keeping only the ones that cross nothing — confirmed by re-running the crossing-pair test many times over
separate process invocations (each with html5ever's own randomized hasher too) with no failures.

**Configuration**: `global_settings` (`ayranTag.tagName`/`ayranTag.idAttr`/`ayranTag.selectorAttr`), kept
in a small in-memory cache (`ayran_tags::current()`, mirroring `appearance.rs`'s own `CURRENT` — read once
at startup, updated the moment the admin-only `set_ayran_tag_config` command writes a change) so
`markdown.rs`'s rendering path — hit on every markdown page a window opens — never touches the database.
**Admin-only** (`get_/set_ayran_tag_config`, `admin.json`, not `user-apps.json`): this decides how *every*
markdown file in the app renders, the same kind of whole-app choice `set_appearance_rotation` already keeps
to the admin-app alone; the generic `set_global_setting` refuses the `ayranTag.` prefix the same way it
already refuses `appearance.`/`logging.`. Settings → "Markdown: the ayran tag" has the three text boxes
(committed on blur, not per keystroke), with the rule and the "blank disables it" note in its own prose.

Rust-tested (`ayran_tags.rs`, `markdown.rs`): a simple self-closing pair, explicit paired tags, no selector
(defaults to a plain `div`), an unmatched tag, three-or-more siblings sharing an id, the same id on tags
