that aren't siblings, nested pairs resolved leaf-first, independent pairs at the same level, a genuinely
crossing pair, the selector microsyntax in every order, case-insensitive and fully custom names, a quoted
attribute value containing `/`/`>`-like text, `is_bare_tag`'s own recognition rules, and — in `markdown.rs`
— the full `render_page` pipeline actually pairing two tags written as their own top-level blocks (not
`ayran_tags::apply` called directly on a hand-built string), an unmatched tag still getting no
`data-line` wrapper, and the feature disabled leaving a literal tag wrapped like any other html block.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop): a real `.md` file with a self-closing pair (`section#main.card`,
a forwarded `data-extra` attribute), a nested pair inside it (`div#outer` → `span.inner`) and one unmatched
marker, opened as a web app through the actual `csuser://` pipeline — the rendered DOM had the real
`<section id="main" class="card" data-extra="kept">` holding the paragraph and list that were between the
tags (each still correctly wearing its own `data-line` for mirror-scroll), the nested pair correctly resolved
(`div#outer > span.inner`), and the unmatched `a-y="99"` tag left as a literal (self-closing-normalized)
`<a-x a-y="99"></a-x>`. `get_/set_ayran_tag_config` confirmed admin-only two ways: refused from the web app's
own window with the same "not allowed on window…" message every other admin-only command gives, and
succeeding from the actual main window; the generic `set_global_setting` refused the `ayranTag.` prefix with
"That setting can only be changed by the admin-app." Settings → "Markdown: the ayran tag" rendered the three
text boxes with the real stored values; editing the tag name through a real React-driven input change and
blur (not a direct backend call) persisted it, and reloading the markdown page picked up the new name at
once; setting the tag name blank turned the whole feature off — the same source re-rendered with every
`<a-x>` shown literally, each back to getting its own ordinary `data-line` wrapper — and restoring it turned
it back on. **Not exercised live**: Android (the mechanism is server-side Rust rendering with no
platform-specific code path, so nothing here is expected to differ) and the crossing-pair rejection and the
`selector_attr` microsyntax's own edge cases beyond what the Rust tests already cover exhaustively.

## PDF conversion and viewing

`docs/features/features.md`'s third "New Feature": convert a PDF to html, convert that html to plain
markdown, and view a PDF in the app. Three independent pieces, sharing nothing but the file — a PDF is
never actually *decoded* by the viewer's own backend path (see "The viewer" below):

**PDF → HTML** (`pdf_convert.rs`) is a **heuristic** conversion, not a layout-faithful one — a real,
bounded limitation the module's own doc comment states plainly rather than overclaiming: a two-column
page, a table, or rotated text may read out of order or run together. Built on **`pdfium-render`**
(bindings to Google's **PDFium** — what Chrome's and Android's own PDF viewers are built on), chosen
over a pure-Rust text extractor specifically for fidelity (real per-baseline text segments and font
metadata, not just a stream of characters), and over **MuPDF** (the other native option) specifically
for its licensing — PDFium is BSD/Apache-style permissive, MuPDF is AGPL/commercial-dual-licensed, which
would have obligated this app's own source or a paid license. PDFium is not embedded in the binary: it's
loaded **dynamically at runtime** (`Pdfium::bind_to_library`) from a prebuilt shared library this app
ships as a Tauri **resource** — the matching binary from `bblanchon/pdfium-binaries` (MIT/BSD-style
licensed, same as PDFium itself) for each desktop platform: `resources/pdfium-win-x64/pdfium.dll`,
`resources/pdfium-mac-x64/libpdfium.dylib` (the *universal* mac build — one file for both Intel and Apple
Silicon, despite the `-x64` folder name matching the other two platforms' own naming) and
`resources/pdfium-linux-x64/libpdfium.so`, named as `libloading::library_filename("pdfium")` —
`pdfium-render`'s own cross-platform convention — expects on each platform.

**Each platform's own resource entry lives in its own config file**, not `tauri.conf.json` itself:
`tauri.windows.conf.json` / `tauri.macos.conf.json` / `tauri.linux.conf.json`, each with just a
`bundle.resources` entry for its own binary. Tauri merges the platform file matching the build target
into the base config (JSON Merge Patch, RFC 7396) — `bundle.resources` is an *object* (`{"source": "dest"}`),
which the merge combines key-by-key rather than replacing wholesale (only an *array* field would be
replaced whole), so this leaves the base `tauri.conf.json` with no `resources` key of its own — the reason
for the split: without it, a Windows build would bundle the mac and linux binaries too (and vice versa),
since Tauri has no platform-conditional filter *within* a single resources list, only whole separate
config files to merge selectively per target. **Only the Windows path is verified against a real running
app** (`cargo run`'s own dev-mode resource resolution, which doesn't go through Tauri's bundler or these
platform config files at all — see `pdf_convert.rs`'s `candidate_dirs`, a `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR`-relative
fallback used specifically because dev mode's own `resource_dir()` isn't reliably this crate's `resources/`
folder); the mac/linux binaries and their platform config files are fetched and wired the identical way,
confirmed only by `cargo build`/`cargo test` passing and the JSON's own validity — not by an actual bundled
build or a run on either platform, which this Windows-only environment can't do.

**Android is done, and live-verified on the emulator** — a real APK build (`cargo tauri android build
--debug --target x86_64 --apk`), installed and run on `quick_notes_pixel_34` (API 34, isolated data folder
per this app's own standing rule — the emulator's app-private storage already held real accumulated data
from earlier testing sessions, exactly the same production-data hazard as desktop; verified isolated before
touching anything). **Not a Tauri resource** — Android has no equivalent of a resource directory to search
at runtime — but an ordinary bundled native library, in its own committed folder (`gen/android/app/
pdfium-libs/<abi>/libpdfium.so`, one per ABI: `arm64-v8a`/`armeabi-v7a`/`x86`/`x86_64`), registered as an
*extra* `jniLibs` source directory in `build.gradle.kts`'s `android.sourceSets` — **not** the default
`src/main/jniLibs`, which is entirely gitignored (it's where Tauri's own build puts this app's *compiled
Rust* library per ABI, regenerated every build, so anything placed there directly would never survive a
clean checkout; Gradle merges every registered source directory into the APK regardless). `init_pdfium`'s
Android branch needs no path resolution at all: `Pdfium::bind_to_system_library()` does a bare-name lookup
(`libloading::Library::new("libpdfium.so")`), and Android's own linker already searches the app's native
library directory automatically for a bare name — the same mechanism that finds this app's own compiled
library, so nothing PDFium-specific had to be taught to the linker. **Live-verified**: the built APK's
`lib/x86_64/libpdfium.so` confirmed present by inspecting the APK directly (not just trusting the build
log), then `pdf_convert_to_html` and `html_convert_to_markdown` both invoked for real over the running
app's own devtools connection (`adb forward` + the same CDP-based methodology used throughout this file) —
the converted html was byte-identical to desktop's own output for the same hand-built test PDF.

**The pdf-viewer system app was also tried on Android, opportunistically, while the emulator was already
up for the PDFium work above** — not the original ask, but real findings worth recording. **Two real
bugs, found live, fixed**: (1) the vendored PDF.js "generic" build throws `Iterator is not defined` at
load on this WebView (113.0.5672.136, from `dumpsys package com.google.android.webview`) — it uses a
JavaScript global newer than this engine has; switched to PDF.js's own **"legacy"** prebuilt distribution
instead (`pdfjs/SOURCE.txt` documents exactly why), built for precisely this kind of older-engine
compatibility. (2) Even the legacy build's own **worker** (loaded in its own separate JS global scope —
running as its own module `Worker`, `pdf.mjs`'s own `new Worker(workerSrc, {type: "module"})`) still uses
`Promise.withResolvers`, standardized more recently than this WebView has it either — and a polyfill
installed in the *main* thread's `Promise` never reaches a worker's own separate global. Fixed with
`worker-wrapper.mjs`, a tiny module that installs the polyfill synchronously then `import()`s the real
`pdf.worker.mjs`, with `GlobalWorkerOptions.workerSrc` pointed at the wrapper instead of the worker
directly. **A third issue was found and left as a known limitation, on the person's own decision**: even
past both fixes, PDF.js's actual page rendering draws nothing — `page.render(...).promise` resolves
without error, `doc.numPages` reports correctly, but the target canvas stays entirely blank (confirmed by
sampling its own pixel data, not just checking for a thrown error) — while a plain, unrelated `fillRect` on
a fresh canvas in the very same page renders correctly, isolating the fault to PDF.js's own drawing path,
not this WebView's Canvas 2D support in general. This matches a known class of upstream pdf.js/Chromium
WebView rendering issues (a `WebSearch` for the exact symptom turned up several open issues of this shape,
including a Chromium-tracked one specifically about pdf.js on old WebView builds) rather than something a
small local patch can fix — chasing the exact root cause was judged not worth open-ended effort right now,
given the actual ask (PDFium bundling for the conversion commands) was already fully confirmed working.
**The conversion commands need nothing further for Android; the viewer's own rendering does, if ever
revisited** — a different pdf.js version, a different rendering strategy (back to the earlier-considered
"render pages to images on the Rust side, shown through `MediaViewer`" design this app didn't choose), or
simply waiting out WebView version adoption in the field.

The module's own algorithm, one page at a time: every text **segment** PDFium hands back (already merged
per same-baseline-same-font run — "Pdfium automatically merges smaller text boxes into larger text
segments," its own doc comment) is grouped into **lines** (close enough vertically), then lines into
**paragraphs** (a gap much bigger than the line's own height starts a new one), then a paragraph's own
tallest font size, relative to the *page's own most common size*, guesses whether it's `<h1>`/`<h2>`/
`<h3>` or a plain `<p>`. **Bold/italic has two signals, not one**: a character's own `font_weight()`/
`font_is_italic()` come from an *embedded* font's `/FontDescriptor` — a bare standard-14 font named by
`/BaseFont` alone (`Helvetica-Bold`, no descriptor) has none, and PDFium then reports a generic weight for
every character regardless — verified live with a hand-built PDF using exactly that construction (not a
hypothetical: this is common in PDFs a lightweight tool produced), so the font's own **name** is checked
for "bold"/"italic"/"oblique" too, either signal sufficing — the same practical fallback real PDF-to-HTML
tools use. **Images are embedded as `data:` URIs** (their own reading-order position — top edge — merged
in among the text paragraphs around them), so a converted page is one self-contained file; `embed_images`
off skips decoding them entirely for a faster, smaller text-only conversion.

**Per-page extraction and mass-convert** are the feature's own explicit second half: `pdf_convert_to_html`
takes `per_page` — off, one combined `<stem>.html` beside the PDF; on, a new `<stem>/` folder of
`page-NNN.html` files, one per page — and `html_convert_to_markdown` (below) takes either **one** html
file or a **folder**, converting every `.html`/`.htm` file directly inside it (not recursively) — the
"mass convert" the spec asks for, built to work naturally on exactly the folder per-page mode just made.
Both refuse to silently overwrite an existing file (an `overwrite` flag the frontend retries with, after
asking — `PdfConvertModal.tsx`, `FilesTab.tsx`'s `convertToMarkdown`). Both commands are ordinary
`user-apps.json` file operations (any window may call them, not just the admin-app — like `fs_write_file`),
resolving `(root, path)` through the same `FsScope` every other file command does.

**HTML → Markdown** (`html_to_markdown.rs`) reuses the exact `html5ever`/`markup5ever_rcdom`
parse-a-tree-and-walk-it foundation `ayran_tags.rs` already established — a real, spec-compliant parser
handles whatever html a person's own file contains, not just what `pdf_convert.rs` itself produces.
**"Plain markdown" means no raw html tags survive except `<u></u>`** (the one thing CommonMark has no
native syntax for at all) — every other recognized element becomes real markdown syntax (headings, bold,
italic, links, images, lists, blockquotes, code), and an element this module doesn't specifically know
(a `<div>`, a `<section>` — including ones `ayran_tags.rs`'s own transform produces) is **unwrapped**:
its own tag never appears, but its children still convert normally. Plain text that happens to contain a
markdown-meaningful character (`*`, `_`, `` ` ``, `[`, `]`, a leading `#`/`-`, a backslash) is
backslash-escaped, since text pulled out of a PDF knows nothing of markdown syntax and shouldn't be
silently reinterpreted as formatting.

**A real bug, found live**: the first version parsed its input as a **body fragment** (`parse_fragment`
with a `body` context, exactly `ayran_tags.rs`'s own approach) — which doesn't expect a `<head>` at all.
Converting `pdf_convert.rs`'s own generated page (a *whole document*, `<!doctype html><html><head>
<style>...` — what `wrap_page` produces, and what a real `.html` file on disk almost always is) put the
`<title>` text and the raw CSS from `<style>` into the markdown as ordinary paragraphs, since the fragment
parser has no head-vs-body distinction to keep them out of the body it builds. Fixed by parsing as a real
**document** instead (`parse_document`, which still handles a bare fragment input just as readily — the
same forgiving HTML5 tree-construction algorithm synthesizes `<html>`/`<head>`/`<body>` around either
shape) and walking only the `<body>` element's own children, found by name rather than assumed depth.
Rust-tested with a regression test built from this exact shape.

**A second real bug, found live, in the frontend**: `PdfConvertModal.tsx`'s and `FilesTab.tsx`'s own
overwrite-conflict retry both called `window.confirm(...)` directly inside an `if` condition — but
**`window.confirm` in this app is not the native, synchronous browser function**: Tauri's dialog plugin
replaces it with an *async* one (`async function(i){return await n("plugin:dialog|confirm",{message:...})}`
— confirmed live by reading the actual function installed in the running admin window) that returns a
**Promise**, not a boolean, directly. An un-awaited call is therefore always truthy (a Promise object is
truthy regardless of what the person eventually answers), so the overwrite branch would have fired on
*every* conflict no matter which button was actually pressed — a real, silent-data-loss-shaped bug, fixed
by awaiting it (`await window.confirm(...)`) in both places. The fix itself was **not** re-verified with a
real native button click — the specific dialog this app's plugin shows (a `#32770`-class window whose
child button control returns no readable text via `GetWindowText`/`WM_GETTEXT`, and which hung a
cross-process blocking `SendMessage`/`BM_CLICK` attempt for over two minutes before the whole app process
had to be killed to recover) resisted the same Win32 button-click automation this codebase has used
successfully elsewhere for *other* native dialogs; the fix is a small, unambiguous await-a-promise
correction whose correctness doesn't turn on anything specific to this dialog implementation, so it's
recorded here as reasoned-and-applied rather than click-verified, honestly, rather than claiming a live
test that didn't actually finish.

**The viewer** (`system/pdf-viewer/`, a **system app** — `system_apps::APPS`, `vite.config.ts`'s
`rollupOptions.input` — like Notes, with exactly the same rights as any web app, no more) is the person's
own choice between the two real designs: render pages to images on the Rust side (reusing `MediaViewer`'s
existing picture-viewing UI almost for free) or **embed a bundled PDF.js** and let the browser do the
decoding — chosen. **Vendored, not a CDN**: `public/system/pdf-viewer/pdfjs/{pdf.mjs,pdf.worker.mjs}` are
Mozilla's official prebuilt PDF.js (Apache 2.0, `SOURCE.txt` names the exact release) — under `system/` on
purpose, not the project root, because Android's own asset resolver (`android_windows.rs`'s
`serve_app_asset`) only answers a system app's own request for a path starting `system/` or `assets/`, and
a plain top-level `public/pdfjs/` would have worked on desktop (Tauri's own embedded-frontend serving has
no such restriction) while silently failing on Android. Only the **core library and its worker** are
vendored — not Mozilla's own prebuilt viewer app (`web/viewer.html`/`viewer.mjs`/`viewer.css`) — in favour
of a small, purpose-built page (`PdfViewerApp.tsx`) using PDF.js's own rendering API directly: `getDocument`,
render the current page to a `<canvas>`, prev/next, zoom. **Deliberately modest next to `MediaViewer.tsx`**
— page navigation and zoom, not that component's full set (pinch, drag-to-pan, full screen) — a real,
working viewer, not a reimplementation of everything `MediaViewer` already does for pictures.

**Opening the viewer on a specific file needed its own window-opener**, `open_pdf_viewer_window` in
`secondary_windows.rs` (modeled on the existing `open_page_window`): a plain web app's own address already
names its file, but a *system app*'s is always the same fixed page, so "which PDF" has nowhere to live in
`location.href` for a freshly opened window. The command sets the tab's `resource_id` **directly**, on
the just-created placeholder tab, *before* the window ever opens — `init_window_tab`'s own rule ("echo the
tab's stored resource id, except for a placeholder that was just created, which is filled in from the
page's URL") only takes the "fill from the URL" branch for a placeholder with *nothing* stored, so setting
it first is what makes the viewer's own `init_window_tab` call get the right file back, the query string
(`?root=...&path=...`) decoded into a `FileRef` and passed to `mediaUrl` — the same address `<img>`/
`<video>` already use for a picture or a video, now `fetch()`ed directly by PDF.js instead. **Always a new
window** (unlike Notes'/User Action's "one reused window" pattern) — viewing a second PDF while the first
is still open is the ordinary case here, not something to replace.

**A real `getDocument` bug, found live**: pdf.js's own API expects `getDocument({ url })` — an *object* —
not a bare string; the first version passed the url directly and got "expected either `data`, `range`, or
`url` parameter" back at runtime (a real API-shape mistake, not a typo TypeScript's own loose local types
for the vendored module happened to catch). Fixed by wrapping it.

**Opening the viewer from Notes — the File Manager and the Note Files Explorer, not just the admin-app's
Files tab** (asked for directly: "I only got to open it from the admin app, but I want to be able to open
it from the notes app too"). `open_pdf_viewer_window` took a plain `(root, path)` pair, which only ever
named a local file (the user folder or a picked folder) — the exact shape the admin-app's Files tab always
had, but not enough for Notes, where the identical click can be on a file of **any** of its sources,
Filen included. The command now takes the same `FileRef` shape `media_url` already does (`storage`,
`root`, `userId`, `branch`, `path`) and builds the tab's resource-id query accordingly — `path` plus either
`root` (`user` or a picked folder's id, as before) or `userId`/`branch` for a Filen file — so `fileFromResourceId`
in the viewer's own `main.tsx` decodes all three storages, not just the first two. `lib/secondaryWindows.ts`
gained `openPdfViewerWindow(file: FileRef)`, called both by the Files tab's `viewPdf` (now building a
`FileRef` instead of passing `root`/`path` positionally) and by a new `viewPdf` in `NotesApp.tsx` — the same
shape `viewMedia` already has, using `source.fileRef(path)` so it works unchanged for the user folder, a
picked folder, a Filen account (in a branch too) and, via `scopedSource`, the Note Files Explorer. A `.pdf`
is now folded into `openEntry`'s own kind-dispatch (`isPdfFile`, `lib/media.ts` — new, alongside
`mediaKindOf`, since a PDF isn't one of `MediaKind`'s own three): a plain click opens the viewer instead of
falling through to "not a default text file," and both the row's **View** action and the card menu's own
entry (`menuFor`) show "View"/the eye icon for a PDF exactly as they already did for a picture or a video —
"Edit as text" stays offered alongside it, same as for media.

**The current page is persisted as app state, per file, across every window and entry point** — the second
part of the same ask. The pdf-viewer system app has **one shared `app_id`** (`system:pdf-viewer`) for every
window, unlike an ordinary web app whose state the backend already scopes to its own file — so `pageStateKey
(file)` folds the `FileRef` itself into the key (`pdfViewer.page.<storage>:<userId>:<branch>:<root>:<path>`),
or two different PDFs opened at different times would silently overwrite each other's remembered page. The
document load and the restore are one `Promise.all` (the file's own page count isn't known until the
document has loaded, so the saved value is clamped against it before it's trusted); a `restoringRef` flag
tells the save effect to skip the one write that would just echo back the value the restore itself set,
so opening a file doesn't immediately re-save the page it already had. **Third part of the ask**: a typeable
"go to page" box (`.pdf-viewer-page-input`) sits where the plain `N / M` indicator used to, between the
prev/next buttons — resynced to the real current page on every change (so a flip by the buttons, a restore,
or a rejected out-of-range value all show correctly), committed on Enter or on leaving the box, Escape
reverts; an out-of-range or non-numeric value is refused the same way, snapping back rather than erroring.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP, a hand-built 3-page PDF with distinct text per
page): opened from Notes' File Manager (the user folder) — real rendered content confirmed by screenshot
("Page 1 of 3"), typing `3` into the new box and pressing Enter navigated to the real "Page 3 of 3" (Next
correctly disabled at the end); closing that window and reopening the *same* file from the File Manager
again restored it to page 3 exactly, confirmed both by the box's value and a fresh screenshot of the real
rendered page; opened from the **Note Files Explorer** (a PDF placed in a real note's `01` files folder,
reached through *Manage notebooks* → a real notebook → a note → *Its files*) opened its own, independent
viewer starting at page 1 — a different file, its own key, no cross-talk with the first; opened from the
**admin-app's Files tab** on the *same* file the Notes session had left at page 3 restored to page 3 there
too, confirming the key is derived purely from the file, not from which surface opened it. `npx tsc --noEmit`
and `cargo build`/`cargo test --lib` (263 tests) all clean throughout.

**A real CSP gap, found live**: `img-src`/`media-src` already allow this app's own web-app origin
(`csuser:`/`http://csuser.localhost`, for Notes' viewer and thumbnails — see "Media") — but PDF.js doesn't
load a PDF as an `<img>`/`<video>` element, it `fetch()`es the bytes itself (in pieces, via `Range`
requests, which `file_serving.rs` already answers for any file), and `fetch()` is governed by
**`connect-src`**, which didn't list that origin — a same-origin-only page (`connect-src 'self' ...`)
couldn't reach a *different* origin's files even though `img-src`/`media-src` already permit exactly that
kind of cross-origin read for other elements. The viewer's very first attempt failed outright with a
CSP-blocked "Failed to fetch". Fixed by adding `csuser: http://csuser.localhost` to `connect-src` too, in
both `tauri.conf.json`'s `csp` and `devCsp`, and updating `the_csp_never_names_an_external_host_or_scheme`'s
own check (which already special-cased `img-src`/`media-src` for this exact origin) to allow it for
`connect-src` as well — the identical "a system app may load user-content" principle, just extended to
`fetch()`-based access alongside element-based access, not a new exception to the policy's own spirit.

Rust-tested (`pdf_convert.rs`, from a minimal hand-built PDF constructed *inside* the test itself — no
external fixture file — with a heading, an ordinary paragraph, a bold line with no `/FontDescriptor`, and
a second paragraph after a gap): heading/paragraph/bold-by-name detection and top-to-bottom reading order
all confirmed from a real PDFium parse (not a hand-rolled parser of my own); `wrap_page`'s shape;
`is_bold`/`is_italic`'s name-fallback rules directly. `html_to_markdown.rs`: headings/paragraphs, bold/
italic/underline, links and images becoming real markdown syntax, lists, an unrecognized wrapper unwrapped,
blockquote/code, markdown-special characters escaped, a leading `#`/`-` escaped, no raw tag surviving
except `<u>`, and the whole-document head-leak regression. **Verified live** (isolated data folder,
desktop): the full pipeline through the real UI — a real `.pdf` file's *Convert to HTML…* action (both
checkboxes) producing byte-correct output (heading, bold line, paragraph order all matching the source);
per-page mode producing a real `page-001.html`; *Convert to Markdown…* on that folder ("mass convert")
producing a correct, head-leak-free `page-001.md`; the overwrite refusal firing on a real second attempt;
the admin-only-vs-web-app-window distinction for `get_/set_ayran_tag_config`-style command gating verified
the same way earlier is architecturally the same pattern these commands don't use (they're plain
`user-apps.json` commands, checked instead against a real web app window succeeding, which it did). The
**viewer**: opened on a real one-page and a real two-page PDF (both hand-built inside this session, the
same construction as the Rust test's own fixture) — real rendered content confirmed by sampling the
canvas's own pixel data (not just "a canvas exists"), zoom confirmed by the canvas's own pixel dimensions
changing, and **page navigation confirmed by both the page indicator and the canvas's rendered content
changing together** between two distinct, genuinely different pages. **Android**, in a later pass (see
above for the full story): the conversion commands (`pdf_convert_to_html`, `html_convert_to_markdown`)
confirmed live on a real emulator, byte-identical to desktop's own output; the viewer opened correctly and
got past two real JavaScript-engine compatibility bugs (fixed), but its actual page rendering draws a blank
canvas on this WebView version — a known class of upstream pdf.js/WebView issue, left as a documented
limitation rather than chased further, on the person's own explicit decision once the actual ask (PDFium
bundling) was already confirmed working. **Not exercised live**: macOS/Linux beyond `cargo build`/
`cargo test` passing there too — their PDFium binaries and platform config files are fetched and wired the
identical way as Windows's, but this Windows-only environment can't run either platform's actual build.

## Editor ↔ syncing web app: scroll and refresh options

Four options, all in **Notes settings → "Editor ↔ syncing web app"** (`notes.settings`'s `syncScrollMirror`/`syncScrollKeyboard`/`autosync`; `NotesSettingsPage.tsx`), for when a note's editor and its web app (opened from it with *Open it as a web app*, above) are open in two windows at once — a genuinely separate webview each, so nothing lets one window reach into the other directly: everything below goes editor-window → backend → the syncing tab's window, the same relay `notify_file_saved` already used, three new commands beside it (`notes_pages.rs`, granted in `user-apps.json` like the rest — nothing here is Notes-only in principle, any window could call them, only Notes' own editor does).

- **Mirror-scroll** (`syncScrollMirror`, **off by default**) — the web app follows the editor's own scroll, continuously, like a split preview. **Line-anchored, not proportional**: `markdown.rs`'s `render_page` wraps every *top-level* block it renders (a heading, a paragraph, a list, a table, a quote, a code block, a rule, a raw-html block — never something nested inside one) in `<div data-line="N">`, `N` its first source line, reusing `pulldown_cmark`'s own byte-offset tracking (`into_offset_iter`) rather than re-parsing or scanning the rendered HTML; a plain `<div>` changes nothing about how a block lays out. `CodeEditor.tsx` gained a generic `onVisibleLineChange` prop (the same shape as the existing `onOpenLink` — CodeEditor only reports *what*, never decides *why*): a `scroll` listener on the frame, throttled to at most once per animation frame, works out the topmost visible **source** line from `scrollTop / lineHeight` (`lineHeight` read once via `getComputedStyle`, always resolved to an absolute px value however it was authored) — the same accepted approximation the line-number gutter already makes and already documents (a line long enough to wrap, when "wrap long lines" is on, occupies more than one visual row, which this plain division doesn't know about). `NoteEditPage.tsx` forwards it, when the setting is on, to `sync_scroll_to_line(file, line)`, which finds the syncing window (`secondary_windows::syncing_windows` — a no-op, not an error, when none is open right now, so the caller never has to track that itself) and sends it `sync-scroll-to-line { line }`; the bootstrap script's own listener scans `[data-line]` for the nearest one at or before `line` and scrolls to its `offsetTop`.
- **Keyboard nudge** (`syncScrollKeyboard`, **on by default** — it needs a deliberate key combination, so there's nothing accidental to protect against) — **Ctrl+Alt+↑/↓/Page Up/Page Down**, pressed in the editor, move the web app's scroll by a step (±60px) or almost a screenful (±90% of its own `innerHeight`) without leaving the editor or touching mirror-scroll. `CodeEditor.tsx` gained a second, equally generic prop, `onScrollNudge` (checked in `onKeyDown` *before* the existing Ctrl/Ctrl+Shift undo-redo branch, so it can't be shadowed by it), reporting only the plain direction; `NoteEditPage.tsx` forwards it to `sync_scroll_nudge(file, direction)` (`ScrollNudge`, a plain four-variant enum — `Up`/`Down`/`PageUp`/`PageDown` — serialized as the bare camelCase string a native JS enum would be, not wrapped in an object), relayed the same way as mirror-scroll's own command. Deliberately **independent of mirror-scroll** — a nudge works whether or not the web app is currently following the editor's own scroll.
- **"Refresh the web app"** (a new button in the editor's header, beside "Open it as a web app") — a deliberate **"start over"**, unlike the automatic reload on save (below): `sync_refresh_without_scroll(file)` sends `sync-refresh-no-scroll` to the syncing window, whose listener sets a `NO_SCROLL_KEY` flag in `sessionStorage` and reloads; the bootstrap script's own `restoreScrollIfWasAtBottom` checks (and clears) that flag *first*, before its ordinary "was it at the bottom" check, so this one load skips any scroll restoration. Resolves to how many windows were told (0 when the note has no syncing web app open right now), shown as a notice ("This note has no open web app to refresh.") rather than silently doing nothing. **This needed a second fix beyond the flag itself, found live**: the webview's own history has its own, entirely separate idea of restoring the previous scroll position across a same-document reload — it runs regardless of what `NO_SCROLL_KEY` decides, so the flag alone wasn't enough (a refresh landed right back where the page already was, `NO_SCROLL_KEY` notwithstanding). `history.scrollRestoration = 'manual'`, set once near the top of the bootstrap script, hands that decision to the script alone on every load — a fresh load then starts at the very top unless `restoreScrollIfWasAtBottom` puts it at the bottom instead, which is also what makes "no scroll" need nothing further than *skipping* that function (there's no explicit `scrollTo(0, 0)` call for it — the browser's own default, with automatic restoration turned off, already is the top).
- **Autosync** (`autosync`, **on by default** — today's behavior, unchanged) — whether saving the note reloads its syncing web app automatically at all. `NoteEditPage.tsx`'s `save()` now checks the setting before calling `source.notifySaved?.(currentPath)`; turning it off doesn't touch "Refresh the web app" above, which always works regardless. The already-existing bottom-pin behavior (this section's neighbor, "Markdown web apps") is unconditional on *every* reload of the page, whatever triggers it — so "when autosync is on and the web app is scrolled to the bottom, saving scrolls it to the new bottom" was already true before this session's changes; what's new is only the *option to turn autosync off entirely*.

Rust-tested (`markdown::tests`): every top-level block of a mixed document (a heading, a paragraph, a list as a whole from its first item's line, a rule — which, unlike the others, has no `Start`/`End` pair of its own and is still wrapped — and a quote) gets its own correct `data-line`, and nothing nested inside one does; the bootstrap script mentions all three new events and checks `NO_SCROLL_KEY` before `SCROLL_KEY`; `history.scrollRestoration = 'manual'` is present. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop) against a real note and a real second window showing it as a web app: the settings page's three checkboxes with the right defaults (mirror off, keyboard on, autosync on); scrolling the editor to a computed topmost line of 77 moved the web app to the *exact* same line's rendered block (`data-line="77"`, both sides independently computed and compared); each of the four keyboard nudges by its exact configured amount (60px, 90% of `innerHeight`), and no movement at all with the setting turned off; the manual refresh button's "no open web app" notice with none open, and, once one was, the real bug above (scroll came back unchanged without the `scrollRestoration` fix, landed at 0 with it) confirmed by rebuilding and retesting; autosync off leaving a JS marker in the web app's page untouched across a save (no reload happened) while the note's own edit was still saved to disk, and the manual refresh button still working (marker gone, new content shown) with autosync off; and, as a regression, the bottom-pin behavior itself (append text, save, autosync on) still landing at the document's *new* bottom, not the old pixel offset.

## Deploying apps

`csdrive-webhost-userapps/<name>/index.html` is source for a small standalone app that the admin-app's Files tab can *deploy*: pick "Deploy apps…" from the toolbar (available in any folder browsable by the file manager, not just the root), choose an app from the list, confirm or edit the destination folder name (defaults to `<name>`, the folder it sits under in `csdrive-webhost-userapps/`; rejected if a file/folder of that name already exists at the current location), and the admin-app creates that folder under the current location and writes the app's `index.html` into it — using the same generic `mkdirRoot`/`writeRootTextFile` root-relative file operations as the rest of the Files tab, not a separate Rust-side "deploy" command.

Each deployable app's `index.html` is embedded into the Tauri binary at compile time (`csdrive-webhost-tauriapp/src-tauri/src/deployable_apps.rs`, via `include_str!`) and exposed to the frontend via two read-only commands, `list_deployable_apps` and `get_deployable_app_html`. Adding a new deployable app means: create `csdrive-webhost-userapps/<name>/index.html`, add an entry to `deployable_apps::REGISTRY` (plus an `include_str!` for its `index.html`), and `cargo build`.

**`example-toolbar`** is the first one (`csdrive-webhost-userapps/example-toolbar/index.html`): a page that binds itself to its tab and draws a small icon-button toolbar of its own — see "The top bar" below for the full, current shape (Back, Suspend, Close tab, Close window, its own ×, and the backend-rendered label/tags/root markup). The file is split into two clearly marked halves: a **library** `<script>` (generic — binds the tab, listens for `tab-navigate`/`show-top-bar`, exposes `suspendThisWindow`/`closeThisTab`/`closeThisWindow`/`goBack`/`setTopBarHidden`; meant to be copied into any page unchanged) and an **example** half (this demo's own toolbar markup and the lines wiring its buttons to the library; meant to be replaced). It is also what the Help tab's "Example: a page's own toolbar" section shows (fetched live via `get_deployable_app_html`, so the deployed file and the displayed one can never drift apart) — see "Help tab" below.

## Help tab

**`components/HelpTab.tsx`** (`src/App.tsx`'s eighth tab, after Settings) is the admin-app's own onboarding page for someone writing a web app of their own: an overview of what a web app is and how to get one running (write files under the Files tab, *Open as web app*, or deploy the sample below), the **essentials** snippet (the whole of `init_window_tab` + `tab-navigate` binding — the minimum any page needs), the **`example-toolbar`** sample above rendered read-only (fetched live, so it's always the exact file on disk), and an **API reference**: every command granted to a web app, as it is actually called from a page — `window.__TAURI__.core.invoke(...)`, never the admin-app's own internal `src/lib/*.ts` wrapper functions (camelCase helpers like `readDir`/`suspendSecondaryWindow`), since those aren't shipped to a web app's page at all and would mislead someone copying them.

**The reference is data, not prose** — `src/lib/apiReference.ts`, an array of categories (Files, SQLite, Windows & tabs, Tags, External web sites, Filen.io direct, the Filen cache, local branches, the app's clipboard, appearance, app state & global settings, picked folders, exporting, dialogs, code snippets, Notes-only helpers), each a list of `{ call, returns, summary, note? }` covering every `allow-*` permission in `user-apps.json` (~130 of them) with the call written exactly as a page would type it (byte payloads shown going through `invoke(cmd, bytes, { headers: {...} })`, matching `fs_write_file`/`filen_write_file`/`save_to_device`'s real shape, not a JSON field). `HelpTab.tsx` renders it as a searchable table (`CodeBlock`/`.api-table`; the search box filters by call, summary and note text, per category, hiding an empty category). **This file is the one place the web-app-facing API is documented for someone outside the codebase — keep it in sync with `user-apps.json` and the commands it grants**: adding, removing or changing a command that's part of the web-app grant means updating (or adding/removing) its entry here in the same change, or the reference silently goes stale. Nothing checks the two against each other automatically.

`CodeBlock.tsx` (a small, reusable, read-only counterpart to `CodeEditor` — for *showing* code, not editing it) renders a snippet through the existing `highlight()`/`Language` machinery (`lib/highlight.ts`, the same one behind the file editors), so the Help tab's code gets the same syntax colours for free, plus a copy-to-OS-clipboard button (`IconButton` pinned to the block's corner, `position: sticky`).

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop): the tab renders with no console/backend errors; the essentials snippet and the fetched `example-toolbar` source both show as syntax-highlighted, copyable code blocks; the search box narrows both the categories shown and their rows (typing "suspend" left exactly the window/tab and external-site entries that mention it); deploying `example-toolbar` through Files → *Deploy apps…* wrote the real file to disk (read back and byte-compared); opening it as a web app, its status line picked up a real `tabGuid` from `init_window_tab`, *Close this tab* suspended the window and kept its entry (`isOpen: false`, matching `close_tab`'s documented behavior for the tab an open window is showing), and — after `reopen_secondary_window` — *Suspend this window* did the same via `suspend_secondary_window` directly.

**The keyboard-shortcuts reference** is a section of this same page (`help_docs.rs`'s `get_keyboard_shortcuts_html`, admin-only like every other Help-tab fetch): `docs/keyboard-shortcuts.md` is embedded at compile time (`include_str!`, the same idea as `deployable_apps.rs`'s sample) and rendered to an HTML fragment with `markdown::render_fragment` (a new sibling of `render_page` that skips the title, the full-page wrapper and the bootstrap script — just the converted markdown) — one document serves both a person reading the repo and the in-app reference, so they can't drift apart. The document's own leading `# Keyboard shortcuts` heading is dropped before rendering (`help_docs::without_leading_heading`): the section already has its own "Keyboard shortcuts" toolbar heading, so keeping the file's `<h1>` too would repeat it. The fragment is dropped into the page with `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` under `.markdown-fragment` (styled to match the rest of the page — `App.css`'s own table/code rules — rather than `markdown.rs`'s `STYLE`, which is for a markdown file opened as a standalone web app). Rust-tested (`help_docs::tests`): the heading-stripping helper on both a heading and a headingless string, and the real document — confirmed to still start with `# ` (the assumption the helper exists for) — renders with no `<h1>` and the real shortcut text still present.

**The page has its own autohiding header** (`HelpTab.tsx`'s `HelpHeader`, `lib/helpHeader.ts`): a `position: sticky` bar at the top of the tab's own scroll (it has to find `.tab-content` itself via `.closest()`, since that's `App.tsx`'s scrolling element, not anything the Help tab owns) with one button, opening a **table of contents** popup (`Modal`, a plain list of the page's own sections — `SECTIONS` in `HelpTab.tsx`, kept in one place so a new section is one entry) whose links `scrollIntoView` the matching heading. It **hides on scrolling down, past a small threshold so it doesn't flicker right at the top, and comes back on scrolling up, on reaching the top, or when told to** — pressing the **Help tab's own head again while it's already showing** calls `showHelpHeader()` (`App.tsx`'s tab button: pressing a tab that's already active normally does nothing, so this is the one tab whose repeat-press is intercepted and given a job). Hiding **collapses `max-height` rather than a `transform`**: a `position: sticky` element still reserves its own height in the layout regardless of where it's painted, so a translate-based hide left a permanent blank gap the header's own height wherever it was hidden — found live, fixed by collapsing height and padding instead, which actually gives the space back. Every section heading gets `scroll-margin-top` matching the header's height, so a jump from the table of contents lands a heading *below* the header rather than under it. **The header is force-collapsed, with a deliberate delay, before a table-of-contents jump actually scrolls** (`goTo`): scrolling the header's own collapse into the same window as `scrollIntoView`'s own animation shifts the very thing being scrolled toward mid-flight (found live: with both racing, the target landed ~140px further down than it should have — collapsing first and waiting the transition's own duration out before scrolling fixed it exactly). Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop): the header's text and TOC button render; the TOC popup lists all six sections and clicking one closes the popup and scrolls there, landing the heading exactly `scroll-margin-top` below the tab's own scrollport (not the window — `.tab-content` sits below the app's own top nav, accounted for); scrolling down hides the header (confirmed the *collapsed* height is ~0, not just visually translated away) and scrolling up — or reaching the top — brings it back; and pressing the Help tab's button again while the header was hidden (and still on the Help tab, not navigated away) showed it again.

## The top bar

The iframe-based top bar of the earlier design is gone for good (see "Why a window and not a frame" under "User Action" for the same reasoning applied there); this is the plain-DOM replacement: **only Notes' own pages get one built in** — not a user web app, and not even a note opened as a web app (a genuinely different page, served over `csuser`, outside Notes' own React tree). A user web app that wants the same thing draws it itself, the same way `example-toolbar` does (see "Deploying apps") — using exactly the same backend support Notes does, so the two stay in step.

**`NotesTopBar.tsx`**, mounted once in `NotesRoot.tsx` (above `{view}`, so every place of Notes — home, notebooks, the notes list, an editor, the file manager, settings — gets it uniformly without each page adding its own copy): **Go back one step**, **Suspend this window**, **Close this tab**, **Close this window** (`closeSecondaryWindow`, already granted to every window — unlike Suspend, there is no "Reopen" after this one), the markup below, and its own **×** to hide itself.

**Starting visibility and persistence** (`top_bar.rs`, `tabs.top_bar_hidden` — a nullable column, migrated in like every other tab column). Absent a persisted choice (`NULL`, the ordinary case), a tab's bar follows the global **`topBar.autohide`** setting (`global_settings`, **on by default** — Settings → Appearance → Top bar's checkbox), so a fresh install behaves exactly as the original, deliberately-partial design did: hidden until asked for (the checkbox is in Settings → "Top bar", with the other two below). Pressing the bar's own **×** persists `1` (explicitly hidden) — it stays closed across a reload, a tab switch back to it, or the window being suspended and reopened, until overridden. `show_top_bar` (below) persists `0` (explicitly shown) — not merely "unclose" back to `NULL`, so it stays shown even if autohide is later turned on, until closed again. Both `init_window_tab`/`add_window_tab`/`tab-navigate` (`TabInitResponse.topBarHidden`) and the live `show-top-bar` event report the *current* answer, so a page never has to compute this itself — `NotesTopBar` simply sets its shown state from `topBarHidden` on every tab bind/switch and from the event.

**The markup** (`top_bar.rs`'s `render_html`, delivered as `TabInitResponse.topBarHtml` and — recomputed from the label just set — `update_tab_resource`'s answer): the same two rows of bullet-separated, styled spans the admin-app's own tab row shows (`AppsTab.tsx`'s `TabTextRow`, mirrored server-side: bold/italic via inline `style`, `mono` via a class), the tab's own tags (unless **`topBar.hideLabels`**) and — for a Notes tab that's showing a source — the root it's in with the root's own tags (unless **`topBar.hideRoot`**; `top_bar::root_of` mirrors the frontend's `rootOfTab` exactly, so the two always agree on a root's guid), all HTML-escaped and rendered **server-side, with no JavaScript** — deliberately no "manage labels" button in it (a page just does `element.innerHTML = topBarHtml`). Its classes (`csdrive-tb-*`) are styled by a **code snippet** (`code_snippets.rs`'s `TOP_BAR_INFO_CSS`, appended to the list every window already applies), so a page gets a consistent look for free without shipping its own CSS for it — reusing the exact mechanism CLAUDE.md's "Android" section describes for platform fixes, now used for shared *look*, not just platform quirks.

**The Back button** (`window_go_back`, granted in `user-apps.json` — every window may call it, not just Notes'): the cross-platform front door to `secondary_windows::go_back`, which used to be Android-only (its hardware Back button, `nativeGoBack`) — undoing one step of tab-switch/link navigation for the calling window. `window_host::navigate_to_url` (new, alongside `navigate`, on both the desktop and mobile platform modules) does the actual navigation from the raw `Url` `go_back` hands back, since that address is reconstructed from what the window showed a moment ago rather than built fresh from a `Page`. `Ok(false)`: nothing to undo — the page's own business what to do then (Notes and `example-toolbar` simply do nothing, same as a second hardware Back press falling through to suspending the window).

**`show_top_bar`** (`secondary_windows.rs`, admin-only — granted in `admin.json`, not `user-apps.json`) sends the `show-top-bar` event to **one open window** (`guid`) or **every open window at once** (no `guid`) via the existing `window_host::emit_if_open`/`emit_to_all`, and — this is what makes it a real *override*, not just a for-now nudge — **persists `top_bar_hidden = 0`** first: for one window, on the tab it's currently showing (`SecondaryWindowsState::current_tab_of`); for every window, on **every tab in the database** (`top_bar::show_every_tab`), open or not, so a suspended window's bar comes back shown too when it's reopened. Two triggers, both in the admin-app:
- **A tab's own row**, in the System/User Apps tabs (`AppsTab.tsx`'s `TabRow`) — a `PanelTop`-icon *Show its top bar* action, offered only while `tab.showing` (the same gate `reload_tab`'s button uses — no open window means nothing to tell), calling `showTopBar(tab.windowGuid)` directly (no backend lookup needed: `TabRecord.windowGuid` is already loaded).
- **Settings → "Top bar" → "Show the top bar for every open window"** — one button, `showTopBar()` with no argument, for when a header is hidden somewhere and there's no reason to hunt down which tab. The same Settings section has the three checkboxes above (`topBar.autohide`/`topBar.hideLabels`/`topBar.hideRoot`), read and written through the ordinary `get_/set_global_setting` like `rowActionsCompact` — no dedicated commands needed for the settings themselves.

**`src/lib/secondaryWindows.ts`** adds `showTopBar(guid?)`, `onShowTopBar(callback)` (`getCurrentWebviewWindow().listen('show-top-bar', …)`, the same "this window only, not the global `listen`" rule as `onTabNavigate`), `setTabTopBarHidden(tabGuid, hidden)` (persists the person's own close) and `windowGoBack()` (the Back button); `TabInitResponse` carries `topBarHtml`/`topBarHidden`, and `updateTabResource` now resolves to `{ topBarHtml }` instead of nothing. **`src/system/notes/topBarInfo.ts`** is a tiny module-level pub/sub store (not React state, since the two things that produce this data — `tabs.ts`'s `registerTab`/`onTabNavigate` handling and its three `report*` functions — sit above `NotesTopBar` in the tree with no other reason to hold a ref to it): `setTopBarInfo`/`setTopBarHtml` publish, `subscribeTopBarInfo` is `NotesTopBar`'s hook.

**`example-toolbar`** (see "Deploying apps") is the reference implementation of "a user app adding its own header of this kind": small inline-SVG icon buttons (Back, Suspend, Close tab, Close window, its own × — a plain html page has no `lucide-react`) in place of the earlier text buttons, `#toolbar-info` holding whatever `topBarHtml` says, starting shown or hidden from `topBarHidden`, its own × calling `setTopBarHidden(true)` to persist the close, and `onShowTopBar` revealing it again — the exact same shape as `NotesTopBar`, minus the window-management difference that Notes' pages always have a tab to close while an arbitrary web app might not need one. The Help tab's API reference documents all of it under "Windows & tabs" (`window_go_back`, `set_top_bar_hidden`, the extended `init_window_tab`/`update_tab_resource` shapes) and the three settings under "App state & global settings", since a page needs to know these exist even though most of them it can only *react* to, never trigger on itself (`show-top-bar`).

Rust-tested (`secondary_windows::tests`, `top_bar::tests`): a fresh tab has no markup and follows autohide; a label produces styled, bulleted markup; closing persists `1`, the global "show" persists `0` and survives autohide being turned back on, and a never-touched tab still follows the (now different) global setting; `root_of` agrees with the frontend's `rootOfTab` for the user folder, a picked folder (by its own label) and a Filen account (by its email), and is `None` for a non-Notes tab or a Notes tab with no source yet; `hideRoot` drops the whole root section while keeping the label. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop): the three Settings checkboxes render with the right defaults and persist; a fresh tab's bar starts hidden; the admin's per-tab "Show its top bar" reveals it live with real markup (a bold label row, a plain second row); closing it (×) and reloading the window kept it hidden; the admin's "show" persisted too — still shown after another reload; the Back button, tested against a real backend tab switch (`activate_tab` between two tabs of the same window), switched back correctly and (confirmed with a JS marker left in the page) did **not** trigger an extra reload; the root section showed "User folder" while browsing it and disappeared live the moment `topBar.hideRoot` was turned on; the CSS snippet's rule was confirmed present and applied via `getComputedStyle`. Verified live on the Android emulator too (real app data, not isolated — a fresh Notes window and tab, `rowActionsCompact` on so "Show its top bar" was reached through the row's own **⋯** menu): the Settings checkboxes, the bar starting hidden then shown live with the same markup, **Close this window** (closed the activity *and* removed its entry — confirmed via `list_secondary_windows` returning none), and `window_go_back` against a real two-tab switch (`add_blank_tab` + `activate_tab`), which correctly returned the window to the first tab — the same mechanism Android's hardware Back button already used internally, now reachable from JavaScript on that platform too and proven to work through the real `android_windows::host::navigate_to_url` path, not just in theory.

## Top bar autohide and nested scrollable content

**Scroll-driven autohide is now shared** (`src/lib/scrollAutohide.ts`, `useScrollAutohide`) by three things that used to be independent (the Help tab's own header) or didn't autohide at all (Notes' top bar, an editor page's own header): a single `scroll` listener on `document`, in the **capture** phase, sees a `scroll` event fired on *any* element in the page even though `scroll` itself never bubbles (capture only needs the target to be a descendant of the listening node, not that the event bubbles back up) — so one listener covers both a page whose own `.tab-content` scrolls (an ordinary long list) *and* a page whose outer area never overflows because it's a fixed-height container with its own nested scrolling region instead, exactly the case reported: a text editor is a header, then a box that is "as tall as its text" and scrolls **internally** (`.code-editor`, see "The file editor") — a listener on `.tab-content` alone, which is what the Help tab's original bespoke implementation used, would never see that inner box scroll at all, since `.tab-content` itself sits at a fixed 100% height and never overflows on an editor page. Each scrolled element's own last `scrollTop` is tracked separately (a `WeakMap`), so switching between scrolling two different areas on the same page never misreads the other's direction. **A newly-seen element defaults its "last" position to 0, not its current `scrollTop`** — found live: defaulting to the current value instead (so the first-ever event for a target always computed a zero delta) silently swallowed the *first* scroll gesture on every element, requiring an unrelated priming scroll before autohide ever kicked in; every scrollable region in this app starts at the top when it mounts, so 0 is the correct assumption and matches what the original single-target implementation got for free by reading `scrollTop` once at mount, before anything had scrolled. The hook returns `[hidden, show, hide]` — `show()`/`hide()` set the state directly for a caller's own trigger, with stable identities (`useCallback`) so passing one as a `useEffect` dependency (subscribing to an external "show it again" event) doesn't resubscribe every render.

**Also shows itself again when the content that hid it stops overflowing at all**, not just on an upward scroll — a `ResizeObserver` attached to every element the hook has seen scroll (lazily, the first time each one fires a `scroll` event) checks on every resize whether that element can still scroll (`scrollHeight > clientHeight`); the moment it can't, there's nothing left for the header to be hidden *from*, so it's shown again rather than staying collapsed until the person happens to scroll up by hand. This is the third part of what was asked for ("auto show back... when the scrollable content changes so that it no longer overlaps the screen") and was not covered by the scroll listener alone: shrinking a file's text a lot (most of it deleted, or a much shorter file opened) changes `scrollHeight` without necessarily firing a `scroll` event with a small enough `scrollTop` to trigger the ordinary "near the top" case, and in fact might not fire any `scroll` event at all if `scrollTop` was already 0 when the content shrank.

**The Help tab's own header** (`HelpHeader` in `HelpTab.tsx`) was refactored onto the shared hook, replacing its bespoke single-target listener — a pure deduplication (the delegated, document-wide listener behaves identically to the old one for a page where `.tab-content` really is the only scroller, which is the Help tab's own case) verified live as a regression: hides on the first scroll past the threshold, comes back on scrolling up or reaching the top, and the table-of-contents' own `goTo` (which force-collapses the header *before* `scrollIntoView` runs, so the two transitions don't race — see "Help tab") now calls the hook's `hide()` instead of a local setter.

**Notes' top bar** (`NotesTopBar.tsx`) now autohides on scroll too, layered on top of its existing *persisted* show/hide (an explicit close, or the global autohide-by-default setting — see "The top bar"): `shown` (persisted) decides whether the bar exists *at all* for this tab — unchanged, still `return null` when closed — and, only while it does, a second, independent `scrollHidden` (from `useScrollAutohide`) decides whether it's momentarily collapsed (`.notes-top-bar-scroll-hidden`, a `max-height`/padding collapse, not `transform` — the same reasoning as `.help-header-hidden`: a `position: sticky` box, or in this case a plain block sitting outside `.tab-content`'s own scroll region, still reserves its height in the flow at a translated position). The admin-app's "show top bar" (`onShowTopBar`, per tab or for every window) now clears the scroll-collapse too, not just the persisted `shown` flag — otherwise pressing "show" while the bar was mid-scroll-collapse would only make it *eligible* to show again on the next upward scroll, not actually show it, which is what "when showing the global top bar from the admin app, the editor's top bar should also be shown" was pointing at for the editor's side of this too (below).

**An editor page's own header** (`.notes-page-header`, doubling as `.editor-top-bar` — the Files tab's own file editor, Notes' File Manager/Note Files Explorer editor in `NotesApp.tsx`, and `NoteEditPage`) gained the identical scroll-driven autohide, each with its **own** `useScrollAutohide` instance. Because all of these — the top bar and an editor's own header — listen to the same underlying `document`-wide scroll events independently, scrolling the editor's own text (`.code-editor`) hides *both* the global top bar and the editor's own header at once with no extra plumbing between them, simply because both hooks see the same event and reach the same conclusion; there is no shared "hidden" state to keep in sync. **Only `NoteEditPage` and `NotesApp`'s editor also subscribe to `onShowTopBar`**, so the admin's "show top bar" reveals the editor's own header alongside `NotesTopBar` — the Files tab has no counterpart "global" top bar (that's a Notes-only concept, see "The top bar"'s own opening line), so its editor's header is scroll-driven only, exactly like the Help tab's. `position: sticky` on `.editor-top-bar` is defensive, not load-bearing today: none of the three editor pages actually let anything scroll *past* this header (`.tab-content` doesn't overflow on any of them; only `.code-editor` does, internally) — it's there in case a future layout change ever makes that true, at no cost while it isn't.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop): the Help tab's header still hides/shows exactly as before (regression-checked after the shared-hook refactor, including the first-scroll fix); a note opened in `NotesApp`'s editor, with the admin's "show top bar for every window" pressed first so both bars started visible — confirmed `.tab-content` itself did **not** overflow (`scrollHeight === clientHeight`) while `.code-editor` did, the exact nested-scroll case reported — scrolling `.code-editor` down collapsed both `.notes-top-bar` (`notes-top-bar-scroll-hidden`) and the editor's own `.editor-top-bar` (`editor-top-bar-hidden`) together from one gesture, scrolling back up near the top brought both back together, collapsing them again and then pressing the admin's "show top bar" brought both back without any scrolling, and — with both collapsed again — replacing the file's long text with a single short line (so `.code-editor` no longer overflowed) brought both back on its own via the `ResizeObserver` fallback, with no scroll event involved at all. The Files tab's own editor was checked the same way (scroll down collapses `.editor-top-bar`, scroll up shows it again); it has no global bar to check alongside it, matching the design.

**Leftover space when hidden — first checked wrong, corrected after the person pushed back with a screenshot.** A first live check only compared the header's own box (height, padding) before and after collapsing — both measured correctly at `0` — and concluded, wrongly, that the report must predate the `max-height`/padding fix above. The person disagreed and sent a screenshot of a note's own window (`NoteEditPage`, opened as a web app): no header visible at all, just the editor's own Undo/Redo toolbar sitting right under the window's title bar — and then a real, visible gap before the text. **The header's own box was never the problem; its *parent's* reserved space was.** `.notes-page-header`/`.editor-top-bar`/`.help-header` all live inside `.tab-panel` (`App.css`, `display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: 12px`) — and a flex container's `gap` reserves that 12px **between** two children regardless of either child's own height. Collapsing the header's box to `0` correctly removes everything it owns, but the 12px of *inter-child* spacing its parent reserves for it is a property of the parent, not the child, and nothing had ever told the parent to stop reserving it. Measured directly: the gap between the header and the content it precedes went from 52px (header shown) to 40px (header hidden, first fix) — the *same* 52px minus a missing 12px, not actually smaller because nothing was overflowing. **Fixed** by giving `.help-header-hidden`/`.editor-top-bar-hidden` their own `margin-bottom: -12px` — cancelling exactly the parent's reserved gap, nothing more (kept in sync with `.tab-panel`'s own `gap: 12px` by hand, the same undiven-constant style `max-height: 60px` above already uses) — with `margin-bottom` added to each base rule's own `transition` so the two collapses (the box's and the gap's) animate together, not one snapping ahead of the other. The remaining ~40px below the toolbar in a text editor is the `CodeEditor`'s own always-visible Undo/Redo row (`.code-editor-toolbar`) plus `.code-editor-wrap`'s own 6px gap — real content, not reserved whitespace, and nothing to do with the autohiding header at all; it's identical whether the header above it is shown or hidden. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP) in both the context first measured (the Files tab's own editor, inside the main window) and the one the screenshot was actually of (a note's editor opened in its own standalone secondary window, via `NotesApp`'s File Manager → *Edit as text*): the header-to-toolbar gap measured exactly `0` once hidden in both, and a screenshot of the standalone window afterward showed the toolbar immediately followed by the editor frame, with no unaccounted-for space above the content — the shape the original screenshot showed missing.

**The shake was real, and still present** — reproduced directly (a `MutationObserver` on the editor header's own `class`, jumping `.code-editor`'s `scrollTop` straight to its maximum): five hidden/shown flips inside about 1.5s. The header sits *outside* the element whose scroll drives it (`.code-editor` is a `flex: 1` sibling below it, not a descendant), but collapsing it still **grows `.code-editor`'s own `clientHeight` by exactly the header's height** (measured live: 568px shown, 628px hidden, a flat 60px every time) — the two are flex children of the same column, so the space the header gives up is handed straight to the box below it. Scrolled all the way to the bottom, `scrollTop` was already sitting at the *old* maximum (`scrollHeight - clientHeight`); the instant `clientHeight` grows by 60px, that maximum **shrinks** by 60px too, and the browser clamps `scrollTop` down to fit — a genuine `scroll` event, with a negative delta, that `useScrollAutohide`'s own direction check used to read as "scrolled up" and answer by showing the header again, which shrinks `clientHeight` back down and, since the collapse animates over 0.25s rather than instantly, repeats the whole thing in smaller steps across several frames — exactly the person's own "hiding/showing changes the scrolling container's height, which triggers another scroll in the opposite direction" diagnosis, just one level removed (a *sibling's* height, not the scrolled element's own).

**A first fix tried exactly what was suggested — a bitflag suppressing reaction to scroll events while the hide/show animation is in flight — and it wasn't enough**, caught live rather than assumed correct: a flat, fixed-duration window reopened right into the *next* echo in the same burst (measured gaps between echoes as long as ~480ms, well past the 0.25s CSS transition itself, so a window sized to the transition's own duration wasn't sized to the actual problem); a sliding window that extended itself on every swallowed event survived a burst correctly, but then **silently ate a genuine, deliberate scroll down made shortly after a genuine scroll up** — found live with the very next test: scroll to the top (shows), scroll to an ordinary position 400ms later (should hide) — it didn't, because nothing about the *timing* of a real continued scroll looks any different from an echo's to a clock with no other information.

**The actual fix needs no clock, no window, no suppression state at all** (`lib/scrollAutohide.ts`): the echo has a shape nothing else does — a negative delta that still leaves the element **pinned at its own maximum scroll position** (`top + clientHeight >= scrollHeight`, within `AT_BOTTOM_SLACK_PX`), which a genuine upward scroll, by definition, moves *away from*. The direction check's "show" branch is skipped specifically when that holds; every other branch (hiding on a real downward scroll, showing on reaching the very top, the `ResizeObserver` fallback) fires immediately, with nothing to time out of. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP): the exact reproduction above (jump to the maximum `scrollTop`) now produces exactly **one** toggle (hide) and settles with no further flips; the previously-broken sequence (show, then a real scroll down 400ms later) now hides correctly every time; a genuine, large (2000px) scroll up made *from* the pinned-at-bottom position still shows the header correctly, confirming the fix only ever suppresses the one specific echo shape and nothing else. `npm run build`, `cargo build` and `cargo test --lib` (250 tests) all clean.

**Re-reported, then re-verified as already fixed by the above.** A later report described the header staying hidden after scrolling up from more than a screen's worth down — read at first as a possible gap in the delta-based direction check (which only looks at the *change* in `scrollTop`, never the absolute distance from the top). Live-tested directly (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP) against the exact shape described: scrolled to 2000px down (several screens on the test window), then a small, ordinary upward scroll of about 100px — the header reappeared correctly, every time. The direction check needs no distance-from-top term at all: `delta < -DIRECTION_THRESHOLD_PX && !pinnedAtBottom` fires on any sufficiently large upward delta regardless of the starting position, so this was already covered by the anti-shake rewrite above and needed no further change. Nothing in the code was touched for this item.

## Tags

Tags (`window_tags` table) are not specific to windows: `guid` is any opaque id — a window, tab group, tab, or, in the Files tab, a file-manager root (keyed `root:<rootId>`, e.g. `root:user`; picked roots are remembered and keyed by their opaque id, so their tags persist with them; forgetting a root leaves its tags in the database, and they reappear if the same folder is picked again). **Everywhere tags appear they must be editable and sortable**, which is why every place renders them through the one shared `components/Tags.tsx` `TagList` component rather than its own markup: click a badge to edit its text/colors, drag badges (or use the arrows in the edit dialog) to reorder, × to remove. Backend commands: `list_tags`, `add_window_tag`, `update_window_tag`, `reorder_window_tags`, `remove_window_tag`; order is the `sort_order` column (new tags go last). Any new place that shows tags should use `TagList` too.

**A row of tags is shown only when the record has at least one tag** (`TagList` renders nothing for an empty list unless it is given `showWhenEmpty`), so a listing isn't padded with empty tag rows. The first tag is added from the record's **details popup** (below), which is the one place that passes `showWhenEmpty` — the "add tag" button — so every record that can have tags has a details popup, and a new listing with tags gets one too.

**Root tags** are the tags of a guid derived from a root (`src/lib/rootTags.ts`): `root:<rootId>` for a folder on this device (`root:user`, or `root:<id>` for a picked one — an opaque id, never a path), `root:filen:<userId>` for a Filen account. Every place that shows the root shows the same tags — the Files tab's root switcher and the **tab items in the System Apps tab**: a Notes tab (its resource id names its source, `rootOfTab`) shows two captioned lines, **Root** (the root's tags) and **Tab** (its own), so the two sets can't be mistaken for each other. Both are editable from there. A tab that shows no root keeps the single tag line. **Group by root** (System Apps tab only, at the tabs level; saved as `systemAppsTab.groupByRoot`): the tabs of the group are shown under a header per root (its name, and its tags when it has any; a button for its details) — a root's tabs are together, the tabs with no root last under "Tabs that show no root" — and the root is no longer repeated on each tab. Sorting by hand is off while grouped.

## Dev Tools and file logging

`docs/features/features.md`'s first "New Feature": Settings → an **"Advanced"** section at the very bottom (a
plain `<details>`, since it's the one place in the app that needed a collapsible section) has a checkbox,
**"Show the Dev Tools tab"** (`lib/devTools.ts`, admin-app-own state — `settings.devToolsEnabled` — not
`global_settings`: only the admin-app's own tab bar needs to see it, never Notes or a web app). On, a **Dev
Tools** tab appears **just before Help** (`App.tsx`'s `tabsFor`), with, for now, one page: **Logs**
(`components/DevToolsTab.tsx`).

**Everything about what gets logged is Rust-only — the frontend is never told what's being logged, only the
current level, the log file's own size/location, and its recent content** (`logging.rs`, `lib/logging.ts`):
`get_log_level`/`set_log_level`, `get_log_file_info`, `read_log_tail` (the file's last ≤200 KiB, so the page
never loads something huge), `export_log_file` (the same "save as"/Downloads flow every other export uses —
`device_files.rs`; not asked to confirm itself, since this is the admin-app's own button, not a page's). All
five are admin-only (`admin.json`), and log **one line each time they're called too** — the Logs page reading
its own state is itself an admin operation, the same as any other.

**The file: `admin/logs/csdrive.log`** (`layout.rs`'s `LOGS_FOLDER`/`LOG_FILE`/`logs_dir`/`log_file_path`) —
inside `admin/`, so it's a **protected path** no ordinary file command can reach (the Logs page is the only
way to see it or get it off the device), and it travels with a relocated data folder and is gone when the
folder is wiped, the same as `data.db` and the Filen sessions file. On Windows that's ordinarily
`%APPDATA%\com.ayran.csdrive-webhost-tauriapp\admin\logs\csdrive.log`; on Android, the same relative place
inside the app's private storage. **Created lazily** — on the *first* line actually written, not at startup —
and **rotated** past 5 MiB: renamed to `csdrive.log.old` (replacing any earlier one) and a fresh one started,
so a `Trace` session left running can't grow it without bound while a full rotation's worth of detail stays
available.

**A plain `log::Log` implementation of our own** (`FileLogger`), not `tauri-plugin-log` — that plugin has its
own JS-visible surface (console/webview logging, configured from the frontend), which would contradict
"solely in Rust" outright. `log::set_logger` makes it the process's **only** logger, at `log::set_max_level
(Trace)` always — the *real* filtering is `FileLogger`'s own runtime-adjustable atomic (`enabled()`), so
raising the level (`set_log_level`) takes effect at once, with no re-registration. **Only our own crate's
targets are ever written** (`OUR_TARGET_PREFIX`) — being the *only* logger means every crate that logs
through the `log` facade reaches it, not just this app's own code, and `sqlx`'s own per-query debug logging
(full SQL text, one line per query) turned up in the file the first time the level was raised past Info,
noise this feature never asked for and outside everything the level table below promises; filtering by
target, before a line is even formatted, keeps it out categorically rather than trying to quiet `sqlx`'s own
logging at the source. Writes are a plain synchronous open-append-close under one `Mutex` (that also guards
rotation) — not a background writer — cheap enough for what a diagnostic logger actually logs, and the
simplest thing that can't tear a line across two files or two threads' writes across each other.

**What each level adds** (Off logs nothing; each level below includes everything above it), and where it's
called from — `require_admin` and `set_app_state`/`set_global_setting` are existing choke points that already
had exactly one call site to add a line to, rather than needing one in each of the many commands that pass
through them:
- **Error / Warn** — Filen request failures (`filen::api`, below).
- **Info** — every **admin-only operation** (`window_host::require_admin` takes a short `operation` name now,
  logged on success — the one choke point all ~20 admin-only commands already pass through) and every
  **notebook added or removed** (`app_state::set_app_state` special-cases the one key Notes keeps its
  notebook list under, `notes.notebooks` — see "Notebooks" — diffing the old and new list by `guid` and
  logging each side's own difference by *title*; `logging::log_notebooks_changed`).
- **Debug** — every **Filen request** (`filen::api::send_json`/`get`/`download_chunk`/`upload_chunk`, the
  handful of functions every Filen operation ultimately calls): one line each, the endpoint (or chunk
  coordinates) and the outcome.
- **Trace** — the same Filen requests again, but **before** sending too, not just after; every **local
  file-system request** (`fs_scope::FsScope::check_in`, the one choke point every `fs_*` command and SQLite's
  own path resolution passes through — the root and the relative path, never the resolved real one, same as a
  refusal); every **tag added, changed, reordered or removed** (`secondary_windows.rs`, by id — never a tag's
  own text); every **app-state or global-setting change** (the same two choke points above — by *key* only,
  never the value, which could be a draft's own text or anything else a settings page keeps).

**Never logged: a file's contents, or the text of a search** — nothing here reads either into a log line in
the first place. What *is* logged where it's useful for finding a problem: names, relative paths, Filen item
ids, keys and counts.

Rust-tested (`logging::tests`): every level round-trips through its persisted string; a logger only writes
what its own level allows, and picks up a raised level at once; a third-party crate's own logging (a fake
`sqlx::query` record) never reaches the file, at any level, and never even creates it; a file past the size
limit rotates instead of growing, and the fresh file after rotating doesn't carry the old size forward; the
notebook diff logic detects an add and a remove from two lists that share one entry. Verified live (isolated
data folder, desktop): Settings' checkbox correctly showed and hid the Dev Tools tab **only after being fixed**
— a first version read the setting with a plain per-component `useState`, so checking it in Settings never
updated `App.tsx`'s own separate copy and the tab never appeared; rewriting `devTools.ts` on the same
module-level-cache-and-listeners shape `rowActionsCompact.ts` already uses (not a new pattern — the existing
one, applied where it was missing) fixed it, confirmed by the tab appearing immediately and surviving a
restart; the Logs page's level radios, file path/size and recent content all reflect the real file; raising
the level to Trace and browsing the Files tab produced real `fs:`/`tag added:`/`app state changed:` lines with
no real path or value in any of them, confirmed by reading the file directly on disk; lowering it back to
Info and repeating the same actions produced **no** new lines (confirmed by an unchanged line count) —
filtering genuinely happens, not just display-side; a hand-simulated notebook list change produced exactly
"notebook added" then "notebook added" + "notebook removed" for the second write, from a real `set_app_state`
call; the export button opened a real native "Save As" dialog. **Not exercised live**: Filen request logging
(no live Filen account in the isolated instance this pass) — covered by the code review of where the lines
were added and by the existing test coverage of the functions they sit in, not by watching real lines appear
for a real request.

## Seeding demo data for the User Apps tab

`csdrive-webhost-tauriapp/src-tauri/examples/seed_demo_data.rs` populates the real `data.db` with demo data for the 9 sample apps in `user/qwer/` (`index1.html`-`index9.html`), for stress-testing the User Apps tab's nested windows/tab-groups/tabs UI at scale. To run it after a reset (or any time):

```
cd csdrive-webhost-tauriapp/src-tauri
cargo run --example seed_demo_data
```

It requires `data.db` to already exist and `user/qwer/index1.html`..`index9.html` to already be deployed (run the app once, and copy `sample-apps/qwer/` into the real `user/qwer/` folder, before running the script). It finds the real data folder itself the same way the app does, including a relocated custom data folder. Layout it creates: one `secondary_windows` entry per app, except `index1.html` which gets 10; the first of those 10 windows gets 10 `tab_groups`; the first of those groups gets 100 `tabs`. Safe to re-run — every row it creates is tagged `seeded:qwer-demo-data`, and each run starts by deleting only rows carrying that tag before recreating them, so it never touches any other window/tab/tag you created yourself.
